# Changelog

All notable changes to this project will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/),
and this project adheres to [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/spec/v2.0.0.html).

## [Unreleased]

### Added

- Istota can now receive Talk messages as they are sent, instead of asking Nextcloud for them every few seconds. It connects to Nextcloud's signaling server — the same one Talk calls use — and is told about a message the moment it arrives, so a reply starts a single round trip after you send it rather than up to a poll cycle later, and the steady stream of requests per room disappears. Off by default and switched on with `[talk.signaling] enabled`, which needs that server registered with Talk; if it is not, Istota refuses to start rather than quietly polling while you believe otherwise. Two things to expect once it is on: Istota shows as present in every room it watches, around the clock, and a second daemon pointed at the same Nextcloud will receive the same messages. `docs/deployment/docker.md` covers the setup and `istota doctor` reports the health of every watched room.

- A chat room can now be told which brain to run, with `!brain` in the room or from its settings in web chat. Only an admin can set one, and only to a kind the operator has listed under `[brain] room_selectable` — empty by default, so nothing changes until an operator opts in. A room that names its brain runs that brain or fails saying why, rather than quietly answering from the deployment's backup: `!brain` on its own reports what the room runs and what it falls back to, and `!brain default` restores both. Switching a room to a brain that reads model names differently clears the room's model default and says so, since the stored name would not resolve under the new one.

- A room that has been pinned to a brain now says so in the chat header, beside the badge that already shows a room's model default and in a different colour from it. It names the brain and nothing else, and clicking it opens the same room settings the model badge does; a room running the deployment's own brain shows nothing, since that is not an override.

- `istota doctor` now reports whether a skill that calls a model on its own — code review is the one today — can actually authenticate. Nothing in the deployment said so before, so the only way to find out was to run a review and read the result. It checks two things separately: that the skill the credential is meant for still exists under the name the code looks it up by, which is what a rename would silently break, and that the daemon holds a credential to hand it. That second half only answers where the environment it reads is the daemon's own; run from inside a task, where the credential is stripped on purpose, it says so and skips instead of reporting a failure. Credential names are reported, never values.

- A scheduled job can now name the brain it runs on, with `brain = "native"` in its `[[jobs]]` table in CRON.md — a nightly summary on one brain and a repo-touching job on another, without moving the deployment default. Only an admin can set one, and only to a kind the operator has listed under `[brain] room_selectable`, the same allowlist a chat room's `!brain` uses and empty by default; anything else is ignored and the job runs as before. A job that names its brain runs that brain or fails saying why, rather than being answered by the deployment's backup, and `!cron` shows the pin beside the job's model and effort.

- `istota doctor` now reports whether the key that unlocks the encrypted credential store is set and long enough. Without it nothing can be stored or read back, and the old symptom was a count of zero configured credentials rather than an error, so a broken install looked like an empty one. It reports presence and never a value, and where it is run from somewhere the key is stripped on purpose — inside a task — it says so and skips instead of reporting a failure.

- A Docker install can now switch a module off for its user. Health and briefings had no Docker setting at all, so neither could be turned off from the stack; both now go through `USER_DISABLED_MODULES`, the per-user opt-out that already existed for skills, which the setup wizard asks about alongside feeds and money. It seeds the user's profile on first boot, after which the web settings are where a module is switched.

- The Docker setup wizard now asks about the Talk signaling server, and asks it early because that answer has a single window: Nextcloud registers the server during its own installation and never revisits it, so both settings have to be in `.env` before the first `docker compose up`. Answering yes generates the shared secret, adds the `signaling` profile, and points the daemon at the server over the container network, which is a different address from the one Talk hands out. It asks for that second address rather than guessing one: Nextcloud itself posts to it from inside its own container, so a URL that only a browser on the same machine can reach would leave the feature configured, clean at boot, and dead. The prompt and the closing summary both say what the alternative is — `occ talk:signaling:add` by hand inside the Nextcloud container — and that registering a server changes call signaling for every Talk user on that Nextcloud, not only for the bot.

- The Docker setup wizard now offers a model for the Claude CLI brain. It asked only on the native branch, so a default install had no way to set one from setup and fell through to the deprecated top-level `ISTOTA_MODEL`.

- `docker/.env.example` now has a `COMPOSE_PROFILES=` line, in the header that documents the profiles, so a `.env` copied by hand behaves like one the wizard wrote. That header also named only browser and location; signaling is a third profile and is listed now.

- Health has a Documents tab, listing every uploaded document with its filename, date, size and the visits, conditions and vaccinations it is attached to. Until now a document was only reachable through the record it happened to be filed under, so finding one meant already knowing which visit or condition that was — and a document whose links were never made, or were removed, appeared on no page at all while still sitting on disk. From the table you can attach a document to another record, detach it from one, move it between records by doing both, and delete it. A counter at the top filters to the documents nothing points at.

- A task can now read and set the Monarch category mapping, with `istota-skill money monarch-category-map list` and `set`. It reaches the money config the way the other money verbs do, scoped to the task's own user, so asking in chat for a category to be filed somewhere else no longer needs the web UI or a shell on the host. Removing a mapping stays an operator command.

- The standalone setup wizard now asks about every module it can switch on. Health, feeds and briefings had no prompt at all, so a local install got all three with no way to say otherwise during setup; each is now an opt-out question beside money, with `--no-health`, `--no-feeds` and `--no-briefings` for a scripted run. A module whose optional dependencies are not installed is skipped rather than asked about, since answering that question would store a decision that outlives installing them.

- The standalone setup wizard now offers an external CalDAV server. `[caldav]` exists so a local install can point calendar at Radicale, Fastmail or Google without a Nextcloud, and no installer had ever written it — the documentation told you to add the section by hand. Answering yes collects the URL, username and password; the password is read without echo, and the config file is written private when it carries one, because that is the one credential with nowhere else to go.

- The bare-metal setup wizard now asks about the developer skill, and asks for a forge token in the same breath. The deploy refuses to run with the skill switched on and neither a GitLab nor a GitHub token set, and the wizard offered neither the switch nor the token, so wanting the skill meant editing the vars file by hand after a run that never raised the subject. It collects the repos directory and a username and token per forge, reading each token without echo. Turning it on and giving neither token says why and leaves the skill off, rather than writing a settings file that cannot deploy.

- The bare-metal setup wizard now asks about four more settings that each had an Ansible variable and a config template line waiting and no question anywhere: the Talk signaling server, which brain kinds a room or a scheduled job may pin, the fallback brain, and the map basemap provider. The signaling prompt says that switching it on without a server registered with your Nextcloud stops the daemon starting, and the closing summary repeats it. The basemap question is asked at install so an operator who wants the keyed provider can give the key then rather than meeting the watermark later; that key is not read as a secret, because the map hands it to every browser that loads it. The fallback question defaults to leaving the answer derived from the brain kind, since any explicit answer replaces a rule the role otherwise works out for itself.

- The standalone setup wizard now runs `istota doctor` before it prints the next steps, and prints anything that failed. Setup used to end at "wrote some files" and now says whether they work. A red check never fails the install — the config, secrets and database are written either way — and the run spawns no subprocesses, so it costs a fraction of a second rather than a probe timeout per binary.

- A bare-metal deploy can now set two things it never could. Calendar can be pointed at a CalDAV server that is not the Nextcloud the bot logs into — Radicale, Fastmail, Google — by giving a URL, a username and a password, and the password travels in the secrets file with every other credential rather than in the config. And the native brain's per-task transcripts now have settings: whether to write them at all, where they go, and the two independent limits that expire them, one for privacy and one for disk. Both sections were live in the product and rendered by nothing, so the only way to change either was to edit a generated file that the next deploy overwrote.

- The example configuration file now documents six sections that were live settings with no mention anywhere: the CalDAV override above, GPS and visit detection, memory search, the devbox container, the browser container, and the native brain's web-fetch tool. Two carry a caveat worth knowing before you set them — the CalDAV password belongs in the environment file rather than the config, and web fetch is offered to admins only whatever its own switch says.

- Money settings has a Transactions section: the rules deciding which accounts an imported transaction posts to, listed in the order they are applied, with a box for trying a transaction against them and a view of what recent imports matched or fell through to `Expenses:Uncategorized`. The mapping existed before and was reachable from no page, and it was Monarch's alone — one rule set now covers a Monarch sync and a CSV import, which used to resolve the same transaction differently, and a rule can match a payee, a note or a tag as well as a category, and skip a transaction outright. The shipped default map and the old category map, account map and exclude-tag filters become rows in it, editable at last, and those three keep working as views over the same rules; `istota money rules` and `istota-skill money transaction-rules` are the operator and agent surfaces.

### Changed

- **Upgrade note:** the web-fetch tool is now offered to admins only. On a deployment running the native brain a standard user genuinely loses it — it reaches the internet from the assistant's own process rather than through the per-task network allowlist, so they had wider access there than the same person had under either Claude brain, and a shared room applies one brain to every member's turns. On a Claude-brain deployment nothing is taken away; what changes is that a standard user is no longer told about the tool, since the two must agree or the instructions name something that is not there. Either way, web search is unaffected, and where a browser service is configured that stays the way to read a page — but a deployment with neither now leaves standard users with no page-reading tool named at all.

- Setting a profile picture is now one control rather than three. The picture itself is the target — click it to choose a file, or drop or paste one onto it — so the dashed upload box and the Choose file button inside it are gone, and Remove is the only button left. The bot icon on the admin dashboard carried its own copy of the old arrangement and changes the same way.

### Fixed

- A Docker deployment using the native brain against Anthropic was running with prompt caching switched off, and paying for it on every turn. The setting has three states — on, off, and unset, where unset means "decide from the endpoint" and turns caching on for Anthropic — and the Docker config generator wrote a flat `off` instead of leaving it unset, so the deployment was forcing the opposite of the intended default. Leave `ISTOTA_BRAIN_NATIVE_PROMPT_CACHING` empty to get the derived behaviour; set it to true or false only to override. The Ansible path was always correct and is unchanged.

- Three settings a Docker deployment ran at the wrong value are corrected. Each was right when it was written and was left behind when the dataclass and the Ansible role moved together, so the Docker path kept a number nothing had revisited. An idle worker now waits 10 seconds rather than 30 before exiting: a parked worker still counts against the limit on how many can run at once, so a long wait holds a slot against other people and rooms, and this deployment allows fewer workers than a bare-metal one, which makes the cost sharper. The 30 dates from when the setting did nothing at all and was never lowered here after it started being honoured. Learned playbooks are now age-pruned after 90 days of not being used, matching every other install shape; a Docker deployment with playbooks switched on had been keeping them for ever. Nothing is deleted on the first run after the upgrade — every existing playbook gets a fresh window, and one still being recalled never ages out — and `ISTOTA_PLAYBOOKS_RETENTION_DAYS=0` keeps the old behaviour. The three places the Docker path states a default now have to agree, and a test fails on any setting where the generator disagrees with the code's own default without a written reason; the deliberate differences that remain, smaller worker counts, encrypted token storage and the forge wrapper paths, each carry theirs.

- Two ways the playbook cleanup could delete a file it was meant to keep are closed. A playbook marked as pinned was deleted anyway if its file could not be read at that moment, because the pin check treats an unreadable file as unpinned — right when the question is whether to overwrite, wrong when the next step is deleting it. And on a filesystem that refuses to update timestamps, the one-time pass that protects existing playbooks recorded itself as done even though it had changed nothing, so the following night's run deleted them by age. It now retries instead, and says so in the log. Both were reachable only where the cleanup runs at all, which until this release did not include Docker.

- Nine settings the daemon reads are documented now. `config/config.example.toml` and the Ansible template between them described every part of the config file except these, so an operator could set them and have them work with no way to learn they existed: the two memory budgets a prompt is trimmed to, the skill-overlay reindex cadence, the native brain's two compaction sizes, the three models the nightly memory passes run on, and `[web] auth` — the one that decides whether the web UI authenticates at all. A test now walks every field of the config tree and fails on one that reaches neither artifact, which is the field-level version of the section-level check added earlier. It counts a key only where it appears under its own section header, since names like `enabled` and `model` recur throughout the file and a looser match would let one documented key stand in for dozens.

- A password or token containing punctuation no longer corrupts the `config.toml` an Ansible deploy writes. Every value the role interpolated into that file went in unescaped, so a `"` closed the string early, a trailing backslash escaped the closing quote and swallowed the next line, and a literal `\n` was rewritten into a real newline — the last of those silently, leaving the daemon authenticating with a credential that was not the one set. It affects far more than credentials: `bot_name`, the map attribution and the forge usernames are free-form on every deployment, while passwords reach this file only when the environment file is switched off. Alias and override names are covered too, and had a silent failure of their own — a model alias named `gpt-4.1` was split at the dot into a nested table and simply did not exist under the name it was given. The escape is applied to every interpolated value rather than to ones that look risky, and a test now fails if a new line in the template omits it. A Docker install renders its config by a different route, which escapes quotes and backslashes but still not a newline or a control character; that gap is unchanged here and is tracked separately.

- `pyyaml` is declared now. Eighteen test files parse the Ansible role's YAML and the compose files with it while `pyproject.toml` named it nowhere, so it reached the venv only because `caldav` depends on it and `calendar` is in the `test` extra — the same failure as `jinja2` and `psutil` a third time, because the guard against it was a hand-written pair of checks per package and saw only those two. Both directions are a sweep over every package the test suite imports now, resolved against what `uv sync --extra test` actually installs rather than against any declaration anywhere — a test-only package parked in the `docs` extra reads as declared while being missing from every lean install, which is where each of these started. The sweep also found `python-dotenv`, needed by an adversarial script and installed by nothing at all, and `starlette`, which the web app imports directly rather than through fastapi's re-exports. Nothing an existing install pulls in changes; the manifest now says what the code does.

- A location lookup that names a place or a saved-area you do not have now reports a failure rather than reporting an error and calling itself a success. `place-stats` and `restore-dismissed` printed the error and exited 0, so the assistant read the step as having worked. Every skill command now answers the same way: an error in the reply means the step failed.

- Your day's location summary now reads the same in the app as it does when you ask the assistant for it. The two were built separately and had drifted: the app worked out how long you spent at each stop and the street or area of a stop with no saved place behind it, then showed neither, while the assistant put a saved place wherever your phone happened to be sitting rather than at the place itself. Both now report all of it, and a day with nothing recorded answers the same way in each.

- Two things saving at the same moment can no longer leave a file half-written. Your accounting ledger, the invoicing work store and an uploaded health document were each staged through a scratch file whose name was the same for every writer in a process — so two edits arriving together, which is what two browser tabs or a sync running alongside a save look like, could write into that one scratch file at once and publish a mixture of both. The staging name is now unique to each save, so one of the two wins cleanly and a reader sees the old file or the new one, never a blend. Nothing about the saved contents changes.

- Setting a numeric brain option to `0` in an Ansible deployment no longer hands back the default. Sixteen settings in the role's `config.toml` template ran the answer through a filter that treats `0` as "not set", so an operator who turned the availability cooldown, the turn cap, the early turn-budget nudge, the soft deadline, the catalog cache expiry or WebFetch's redirect following off got them all back on, with nothing in the play or the rendered file to say so. Each was audited against the code that reads it: the ten where `0` carries a meaning now render the operator's answer, and the six where it reaches no branch at all — the two timeouts, the output-token cap and WebFetch's two size caps — keep the substitution and say in the template why, so a deliberate one and an overlooked one no longer look alike. `defaults/main.yml` names what `0` means for each. Two booleans in the same block, `allow_http` and `require_url_provenance`, were on the same filter and are plain references now too; neither could discard an answer, since the value it substituted was the one it was given.

- A `[caldav]` or `[brain.native.session_log]` block in an Ansible deployment's `settings.toml` now reaches the host. The converter that turns that file into deployment variables had no entry for either section, so both were dropped without a word and the install rendered the empty defaults — which for CalDAV means calendar quietly stayed on Nextcloud, and for session logs means the transcript settings were whatever the role shipped. Both were only ever settable by writing the underlying variables into inventory by hand. A new check requires every setting of a section the converter claims to cover to survive the whole chain, so the next section added to the deployment cannot be half-wired the same way.

- A password or token in an Ansible deployment's `settings.toml` that contains a quote or a backslash now survives the install. The converter that turns that file into deployment variables quoted every value but escaped only some of them, so a credential with a `"` or a `\` anywhere but the first character produced a variables file the installer could not read — and one containing a literal backslash-n was accepted and silently rewritten with a real line break in it. Every credential in that file went through this: the encryption key, the Nextcloud app password, the mail passwords and the forge tokens. A deployment whose credentials happen to be alphanumeric was unaffected.

- The variables file the installer generates is no longer world-readable. It holds every credential the install was given and was written at whatever the umask allowed, then left on the host; it is created private now. Existing files are not changed — narrow `/etc/istota/vars.yml` to `0600` by hand on a host that has already been installed.

- A deploy that renders a broken `config.toml` no longer replaces the working one first. The role wrote the file and validated it afterwards, so a variable left empty in inventory produced a file the daemon cannot parse, sitting where the previous good one had been — and the play stopping there does not stop the auto-update cron or a reboot from restarting the services against it. The render is parsed before it is moved into place now, so a bad one fails the deploy and changes nothing on the host.

- An Are.na or Tumblr feed added with a stray slash or a pasted channel URL is no longer a subscription that can never fetch. The identifier was stored exactly as typed and dropped straight into the API path, so `arena:/some-channel` asked for `channels//some-channel` and got a 404 on every poll — indistinguishable from a channel that had been deleted. A feed URL is now put in canonical form wherever one is stored, a pasted `https://www.are.na/…` or `https://….tumblr.com/` address is read for the channel or blog it names, and an identifier with nothing usable left is refused rather than stored. Subscriptions already on file need no repair and keep their entries and read state: the same rule is applied again when the poll builds its request, so they start fetching on their next poll.

- A feed identifier is now escaped where it is put into an Are.na or Tumblr API URL. It went in as typed, so an identifier holding `..` or a `?` could move the request to a different endpoint — and because a query is replaced rather than appended, that failed silently with a 200 carrying the wrong body rather than erroring. An ordinary channel slug or blog name is unaffected.

- The health Documents view failed to load with "Health API error: 422" and showed nothing at all, however few documents were stored. It asked for a page of a thousand records from each of the four lists it needs, which is more than the visits and conditions lists will return in one request, and the two rejections failed the whole page rather than just their own part of it.

- The health Documents view now says when the visits, conditions or vaccinations it offers are only part of what is stored. It said so already for the documents themselves, but the other three lists were read the same way and their result thrown away, so the attach picker could be short with nothing to explain it — and a record missing from the picker looks exactly like one that was never recorded. The page names which list was cut and the picker repeats it for the type it is showing, though it takes tens of thousands of records in one list to reach.

- The standalone setup wizard read the IMAP password with an ordinary prompt, so it was echoed to the terminal and left in shell history whenever the wizard was driven from a pipe. It is read without echo now, like the API key beside it. The wizard also asks for the SMTP host rather than forcing it equal to the IMAP host, defaulting to that host so the common case stays one keystroke.

- A yes/no question in the standalone setup wizard now re-asks when the answer is neither, instead of reading anything it did not recognise as "no". On a question whose default is yes that took you the opposite way from the `[Y/n]` it had just printed, so typing `1` or `yeah` switched a module off and stored that.

- The standalone setup wizard now creates the two directories it names in the config it writes. `db_backup_dir` and `temp_dir` were written as settings and made by nothing, so a fresh install had two configured paths that did not exist; both are created private, for the database snapshots and the per-task files they hold.

- The web service can now authenticate to the model on a Docker install. It calls one on three request paths of its own — reading a lab panel, encounter or immunization document, explaining a biomarker, and running a shared briefing block on demand — and the stack passed it only the native brain's key, so on the default Claude CLI shape all three failed with no credential. Both Claude credentials now reach it.

- `browse` now works on a Docker install whose `.env` does not name the browser container. The example file gave the right address while the compose and render fallbacks behind it gave the istota container's own loopback, where nothing listens, so commenting out that one line broke browsing with nothing pointing at a cause. A new test holds all three files to one value for every setting that states a default.

- The Docker setup wizard now writes down a "no". Answering no to GPS location tracking left the example file's `true` in place, so the module was on and its tab present with no receiver container behind it; the same held for the browser on a host that cannot run it. The developer question is now two — whether to run the skill at all, and whether to hand it forge tokens — because git and worktrees need no token and the old single question could not decide both.

- A local install now generates the key its encrypted credential store needs. `istota setup` wrote none, so storing a Garmin, Monarch, ntfy or Google Workspace credential failed on every local install and nothing said why. Re-running setup with `--force` keeps whatever key is already there rather than making a new one, since replacing it would leave every credential stored under it permanently unreadable, and the secrets file is now created private instead of being made private a moment after it is written.

- Shared briefing blocks can be written on a local install again. Writing shared content requires a named admin, and a local install had no admins file for anyone to be named in, so nothing could be written and the failure looked like a missing feature. Setup now writes one naming the local user; an install made before this change is covered by an exception for the single-user shape, which stays narrow — a deployment with Nextcloud storage and its web login switched off is not that shape and gains nothing.

- The two-minute auto-update on a server install now rebuilds the web UI when a commit touches it. It shipped the frontend sources and restarted every service without ever running the build, so a frontend-only change landed on the host and changed nothing a browser saw until somebody ran the Ansible play by hand. The build runs only when `web/` actually changed, reinstalls dependencies only when the lockfile did, and happens before the migrations and the restarts — a build that fails leaves the previous deployment intact rather than new services against an old page.

- An Ansible deploy and the auto-update can no longer build the web UI at the same time on a server install. The auto-update already refused to run twice at once, so a run of commits in quick succession never started a second build — but a play run by hand took no lock at all, and its dependency install wipes the frontend's packages, so landing it mid-build pulled the tree out from under one. The play now waits for an update in progress instead, and gives up loudly rather than proceeding if one has been running for fifteen minutes.

- A server install no longer sits on a half-applied update indefinitely. The update ran `git reset --hard` before anything that could fail, so a failure part-way through left the checkout at the new commit and every later run deciding there was nothing to do — with the reason only in the update log. It now records the revision a run finished, and compares against that, so an interrupted update is retried on the next tick. The first run after this ships records where the host already is rather than redeploying it.

- `istota doctor` now reports a web UI that is out of date with the checkout it is served from. The existing check asks only whether the page exists, which stays true across a stale build, so nothing on the host reported the condition above. Builds made by the play or by the auto-update record the commit they came from, and doctor warns when the frontend has changed since. It asks whether the frontend changed rather than whether the commit did, so an ordinary backend deploy — which correctly leaves the build alone — is not reported. A build made anywhere else, such as a container image or a package install, carries no commit and is skipped rather than guessed at.

- Right-clicking an image open in the feeds lightbox no longer closes it, so the browser's own menu — save the image, copy it, open it in a tab — works again. Tapping the image to dismiss it is now immediate rather than pausing first, which had made it visibly slower than tapping beside the image. Double-tapping no longer zooms: pinch, the trackpad and the `+` / `-` / `0` keys all zoom already, and the double tap was the only thing the pause was there for.

- A room's model default is no longer thrown away when the operator stops allowing that room's brain. A room pinned to a brain that is later removed from `[brain] room_selectable` runs the deployment's brain instead, which is intended — but a model chosen in that room afterwards was then discarded on every turn, because the room still named the old brain and the stored model was checked against that brain's vocabulary rather than the one it was actually chosen for. The room ran the wrong model with nothing said. Istota now records which brain's vocabulary a model was chosen in at the moment it is set, so the check reads a fact instead of guessing from the room's brain. The same fix covers a model set from web chat in a room whose next message arrives over Talk, where a deployment routing those two to different brains disagreed with itself. Rooms whose model predates this keep behaving exactly as before until the next time their model is set.

- Two more places that pinned a model now do it in the vocabulary of the brain that will run it. The terminal REPL's `/model` resolved the name against the deployment's default brain rather than the one a `repl` task routes to, so on a deployment using `[brain.source_type_overrides]` it stored a name the brain that ran the turn could not use and passed it to the endpoint anyway. And a subtask inherited its parent's model even where the two run on brains that read model names differently; it now runs the routed brain's own default instead, which is what happened before the pin was read this way.

- The warning about an ignored brain pin is no longer repeated on every run. A room or scheduled job naming a brain the operator has not allowed is still reported the first time, which is what tells an operator their pin is being ignored, but nothing about that changes between runs — so a job on a one-minute schedule wrote the same line around 1440 times a day, pushing the rest of the log out of the admin Logs pane and filling disk. It is now said once per distinct problem for as long as the daemon runs, and the same applies to a brain kind nothing can build, whether it came from a pin or from `[brain.source_type_overrides]`.

- A model name pinned on a task is now read in the vocabulary of the brain that will actually run it. On a deployment that routes some work to a different brain than its default, through `[brain.source_type_overrides]`, a scheduled job asking for `smart` had that name turned into the *default* brain's model — which the brain that then ran the job could not use, so it was discarded and the job ran on that brain's own default with nothing saying so. Both halves are corrected: a job's model is resolved against the brain the job resolves to, and the check that decides whether an already-stored name still means anything asks the same question. **Upgrade note:** on such a deployment the model recorded against a scheduled job's task changes, and on every other lane — email, briefings, heartbeat checks, web — a model pin that used to be thrown away as unrunnable is now kept and used. A subtask is the exception: it takes its parent's pin only where the parent's lane and the subtask lane read model names the same way.

- An effort written onto a scheduled job's model, as in `model = "opus:high"`, now reaches the run. The part after the colon was dropped when the name was resolved and nothing carried it forward, so the job ran at the model's default effort and nothing reported the loss. A job that also sets `effort` keeps its own value, which is the precedence everywhere else.

- A room's model can now be chosen at the same time as its brain. Switching a room to a brain that reads model names differently used to grey out the model and effort pickers and tell you to come back after saving, because the old model was cleared as part of the switch. The pickers now stay usable and list the models the brain you just picked can actually run, so one save sets both.

- A model choice is no longer thrown away when Istota falls back between two brains that speak the same model names. The Claude Code and tmux brains run the same tool and accept the same models, but a fallback between them dropped whatever model the task had asked for and told the user their choice had been discarded — for no reason, since the backup brain could have run it. A fallback now only drops a model when it genuinely crosses to a brain that would not understand it, which is the case that rule was written for.

- Each brain now uses the model and effort it was configured with. The top-level `model` and `effort` were the Claude Code brain's own defaults left over from when there was only one brain, and because they sat at the top of the file Istota applied them to whichever brain ran — so a room pinned to the native brain, or a task routed to it, ran the Claude model against the native endpoint and was billed per token for it, while the `[brain.native]` model the operator had set was never reached. There was no way to avoid this: the top-level key was the only place to set Claude Code's model. Set `model` and `effort` under `[brain.claude_code]` and `[brain.tmux]` instead; the old keys still work and are moved onto both of those blocks with a warning, so an existing deployment keeps its behaviour without any change. They are deliberately never applied to `[brain.native]`, whose endpoint speaks different model names. **Upgrade note:** a deployment running the native brain that set a model only at the top level and never set `[brain.native] model` will now send no model at all, which most endpoints reject — the old key was reaching that brain only because of the defect, and it cannot carry to an endpoint that speaks different model names. Istota says so once at startup, naming the key to set; set `[brain.native] model`.

- A task that pinned no model of its own no longer loses one when Istota falls back to its backup brain. The deployment's default stood in as if the user had chosen it, so the backup brain refused to carry it, ran on its own model instead, and told the user their model choice had been dropped — about a choice they never made. With nothing pinned there is nothing to drop, and the backup brain simply uses its own default, quietly.

- The native brain works again on a Linux install whose Python environment is reached through a symlink, which is how the Ansible role lays one out. Its tools run in a sandbox of their own, started with the interpreter Istota itself is running under; the sandbox was given the directory the link points at while the interpreter was asked for by the name of the link, which is not a path that exists in there. Every native-brain task failed at start-up with a connection reset, including on deployments that name it only as the backup brain, where the failure appeared solely when the primary was unavailable.

- The same start-up failure is also fixed for an install whose Python was downloaded by `uv` rather than provided by the distribution. The sandbox carries the environment's own directory, and inside it the program that starts Istota's tools is a link to the Python it was built from — so where that Python lives outside the system directories, nothing carried it in and the link pointed at nothing. That directory is now carried in as well, read-only, and only where it is needed: a deployment using the distribution's Python is unaffected, byte for byte.

- Checking chat rooms for new messages can no longer wedge everything else Istota does over the network. That check writes to the database while waiting on Nextcloud, and anything else running alongside it that wanted to write would have stopped the very thread the check needs to finish, leaving nothing moving for thirty seconds. Those writes now take their turn one at a time, and a wait on a writer outside them is given up after two seconds instead of thirty, with the cycle retried a few seconds later. Nothing runs alongside that check today, so this closes the hazard ahead of the work that would have met it.

- Istota can no longer act twice on the same chat message. The mark it keeps of how far it has read each room could be moved backwards by a write arriving out of order, and anything after that mark is then read a second time — a `!command` run again, a confirmation answered again, an acknowledgement posted again. The mark now only ever moves forward, which is the only direction a Talk message id ever goes.
- `istota doctor` no longer reports on a deployment it cannot see. A run that finds no configuration file falls back to built-in defaults and said so nowhere, so every check answered about a database, a temp directory and a security posture that were never configured, while reading exactly like a run about the real install — and from inside a task that was the only possible outcome, since the configuration directory is deliberately out of reach in there. Doctor now says in one line that this run cannot see the deployment's configuration and prints nothing else, treating it as a fault everywhere except in a task, where the boundary is working as intended. Checks about the image rather than the deployment (`--scope image`) are unaffected, since those are the ones a configuration file was never needed for.

- `istota doctor` no longer calls a database file with no tables in it healthy. SQLite reads both an empty file and a header-only one as a valid empty database, so the integrity check passed them and reported them clean; either is an interrupted setup or the wrong file, and both are now reported as such with the same advice the missing-file case already gives.

- A table in web chat no longer squashes its columns down to a character or two. Where a short label column sat beside a long one, the labels were given so little room that words broke in the middle — `Check` rendered as `Che` / `ck` — and the sideways scroll such a table is meant to fall back on could never engage, because breaking words at any letter let the table shrink to fit any width at all. Columns now have a minimum width and words break only where a word can break, so a table too wide for the screen scrolls instead of collapsing; the same applies to tables in the briefings reader.

- `istota doctor` no longer reports a working sandbox as broken when a task runs it. The check asks whether a confinement namespace can be created here, and from inside a task's own sandbox that question has no answer — a second namespace can never be created in there — so it said every task ran unconfined and doctor exited with a failure, on a deployment whose boundary was in place. It now says the question cannot be answered from there and skips, while a task on a deployment that really is unconfined still gets the failure. Two neighbouring findings, about where the assistant's transcripts and each task's prompt files sit, were wrong the same way and now report the question as unanswered rather than as an exposure.

- Code review works again. Every review came back as skipped with a failure about a second after it started, because the credential the reviewers authenticate with was being removed from the environment they run in. It was removed on purpose, to keep it away from the model, on the reasoning that no skill needed it — code review does, and it is the only one that does, so it now gets it back and nothing else does. The credential still cannot be asked for by name over the skill socket. Deployments authenticating with an API key rather than a subscription had the same failure for longer and are fixed by the same change.

- Code review now works on a deployment that reaches the model API through an outbound proxy, a private certificate authority, or a gateway. All three are configured in Istota's own environment and none of it was passed on to the review program, so after the credential fix above it would authenticate and then fail to open the connection or fail the certificate check. Where the certificate authority is concerned every skill program gets the setting, since trusting an extra authority can only help; the proxy and gateway settings go to code review alone, because a proxy address redirects whatever is given it — handing it to a skill that talks to a service on this same machine would send that internal call out to the proxy, which refuses it. Deployments reaching the internet directly are unaffected, since there is nothing to carry. Applies wherever the skill proxy is switched on, which is the default and is required wherever the sandbox is.

- A failed code review now says what the reviewer said. The result quoted only a status word, which is the same word whether the reviewer had no credential, no route to the model, or a model name it did not recognise; the sentence it actually exited on was thrown away. It is now included, on one line and capped.

- Leaving a room now stays left. Two separate things put you back in one. A deploy re-invited you to any default room it had made for you that it found you were no longer in, because a room whose only remaining member is the bot looks the same whether you walked out of it or the original invite never landed — so `general`, which nothing else protects, came back on every deploy. Istota now records whether each invite actually landed, and only retries a room whose last invite is recorded as having failed; a room you left is left alone, and is reported as one you are not a member of rather than as an ordinary existing room. Rooms recorded before this change carry no such record and are read as having succeeded, so leaving works from the next deploy. The cost, for operators: a room whose invite failed before the upgrade is no longer retried on its own and no longer fails the deploy — it is named in the provisioning output instead, and `docs/reference/cli.md` gives the one command that puts it back in scope.

- Deleting a web chat room that had been opened in Talk now hides it for you instead of appearing to delete it. Such a room is a Nextcloud conversation shared with whoever else is in it, and the delete removed every local trace of it — including the record that you had hidden it — while sending nothing to Talk, so the next room check found the conversation, registered it afresh, and the room reappeared. It is now hidden for you alone, the way a room that started in Talk already was: the conversation, its history and the other participants are untouched, and it stays hidden until you post in it again. Leaving the Nextcloud conversation as well is still what removes you from it. Two consequences of that: such a room can no longer be destroyed from web chat, including one whose Talk conversation has since been deleted in Nextcloud — "Reconnect to Talk" is what repairs that one — and a room of this kind that you had archived before this change is now correctly restored when you un-archive it, rather than staying hidden with nothing left to bring it back.

- Checking chat rooms for new messages no longer holds up everything else that writes to Istota's database. When the check needed to ask Nextcloud something — which room a conversation is, where it last got to, what was said in it before — it kept a write lock open for the whole round trip, so any other part of the daemon that needed to write queued behind it, each for up to thirty seconds. Reads were unaffected, which is why it went unseen. Istota now reads what it needs, closes the connection, asks Nextcloud, then reopens to write, and it re-checks anything another writer could have changed in the meantime rather than writing over it. How much this was costing in practice is not known: the measurement added alongside it has so far recorded no such wait on the deployment it runs on, because every room there is already known and settled and none of the branches that ask Nextcloud came up. The later half of the same poll, which files the messages that came back, still waits with its connection open — it has to record a message and move the read position together or messages can be lost — and is left alone until there are numbers for it.

- A Monarch category with punctuation in its name no longer breaks the whole ledger. The sync built an account name for an unmapped category by deleting spaces and leaving everything else, so `Internet Services (Reimbursed)` became `Expenses:Uncategorized:InternetServices(Reimbursed)`, which beancount cannot parse; because nothing checked the name before the transaction was written, every later read of that ledger failed rather than just the one entry. Names are now reduced to the characters beancount accepts, keeping accented and non-Latin letters instead of dropping them and raising a lowercase initial, which beancount rejects — so a category like `misc` now posts to `Expenses:Uncategorized:Misc` rather than to a name no ledger could parse.

- Monarch mappings and profile accounts are now checked where they are set, rather than when the sync next runs. A category mapping, an account mapping, a profile's default account and the global sync accounts all name an account the sync writes into the ledger unexamined, and each was stored exactly as typed; the web UI and the CLI now report the error at the point of entry. A legacy config file holding one is skipped with a warning instead of leaving the money module unusable, and the refusal is remembered against that file so it is not re-derived on every money call; correcting the file imports it on the next run.

- Deleting a chat message now removes its copy in Talk. The removal was made over a connection the web process shares with the rest of the assistant, which belongs to a different part of the process and cannot be used from a web request — so the request to Nextcloud went out and the reply could never be read. Because that reads as a failure, the log then said no credential could make the deletion, for one Nextcloud may well have made. The web process now opens its own short-lived connection for this and closes it afterwards, and when a deletion really does not go through, the log says whether Talk refused it or something went wrong, rather than reporting both the same way.

- Deleting a chat message no longer leaves a connection to Nextcloud open behind it. Removing the message's copy in Talk opened a fresh connection every time and never closed it, so a web process that had been running a while held one per delete. It reached only deployments with per-user Nextcloud token storage switched on (`[web] token_storage = "encrypted"`, with the web token key set) and a live token stored for the person deleting; anywhere else the removal goes out over the bot's shared connection, which nothing here opens.

- A chat reply that fails to send because Nextcloud was slow to answer is now tried again instead of being dropped. On the production server this happened about twice a day: the request timed out, the reply was thrown away, and the only trace was one line in the log. Istota now makes up to three attempts, a second and then three seconds apart, and gives up early if the sequence has already taken too long — a reply that cannot get out must not hold up the work queued behind it. Before sending again it re-reads the room and looks for the message it was trying to post, because a request that timed out may still have been received and written, and sending again would leave the same reply in the room twice. Every message Istota posts carries an identifier it can recognise, and it checks who wrote the message as well, so one somebody else wrote cannot claim to be its own. If the message is already there it is treated as sent; if the room cannot be read, the reply is not sent again, which is what happened before this change anyway. A reply long enough to be split across several posts is deliberately not re-read this way, because the identifier names the whole answer rather than each piece and a partly-sent answer cannot be told from a complete one; those keep the old behaviour unless the connection never opened at all, where nothing can have been stored. A refusal is not retried either: if Nextcloud says the room is gone, or that Istota may not write to it, asking again a second later gets the same answer.

- An answer that could not be posted to a Talk room is no longer lost in silence. If Nextcloud timed out or refused the final post, the failure was logged once and discarded: the task was marked completed, and nothing told the user their answer had gone nowhere. Such a message now goes to the notification bell and is sent as an alert, carrying the text itself so it survives the failure. This covers a scheduled job's and a briefing's output as well as a reply, and it covers the apology for a task that failed — in each case the user was otherwise told nothing at all. The task's own status is left as it was, because what failed is one delivery leg rather than the task. A room the message is only mirrored into is deliberately left alone: for a chat started on the web or by mail the answer is already where it was asked for, and warning about the copy would be a false alarm.

- A request waiting on your confirmation no longer goes unasked when the chat post carrying the question fails. The question is recorded in the notification bell whichever way it is delivered, and the bell entry is deliberately held back when the chat message is the thing carrying it — so a post that never arrived left the recorded question undelivered and the request expired two hours later having asked nobody. The held entry is now sent when the post fails, and it is the actionable one, rather than a second copy that would close itself as soon as it was seen.

- An emailed request whose answer reached neither the mailbox nor the chat room is no longer silent on both. The notice for a failed email reply was skipped whenever the request also had a room to fall back on, on the assumption that the room post had already landed. It had not necessarily landed, and with both legs down that assumption suppressed the last notice there was.

- A user name that does not name a directory of its own can no longer widen what a task can reach. Per-user folders — inside the assistant's storage, and inside its scratch area — were addressed by joining the name onto the path, and an empty name, a `.`, or a full path does not produce a folder below it. An empty one collapses to the folder holding *every* user's folder, which was then made available to the task, writable; for the scratch area that folder also holds each running task's assembled instructions, so one task could read and rewrite another's. The same collapse reached the file tools, the places an attachment may be read from, the folders a skill may write to when it runs outside the sandbox, and the folders the memory search may index and later quote back. Every one of those now checks that the name really does name a folder of its own, and reaches nothing at all when it does not; a sandbox that cannot be scoped is refused outright rather than built around the shared folder. As far as could be traced, nothing arriving over chat, mail or the web could produce such a name — each already checks the sender against the configured users — so this was reachable from the local command line, or from a mistyped user name in the configuration. Creating a task with one is now refused, with the refusal costing that one task rather than everything else queued in the same pass.

- A folder shared with the assistant as read-only is no longer writable by the built-in agent loop's file tools when it sits inside another folder shared read-write. The command tool always honoured the read-only setting, because the sandbox applies the narrower permission last; the file tools worked from a separate list that recorded the folder as merely readable and let the enclosing read-write share decide. The two are now derived from one answer, so they cannot disagree.

- A room that started on the web and was later opened in Talk shows its progress again. The "working on it" line was addressed using the room's internal identity rather than its Talk address, so Nextcloud rejected it, and every progress update after that quietly did nothing because there was no message left to edit. The answer itself always arrived — final delivery resolves the room correctly and always did.

- A room whose Nextcloud Talk conversation was deleted can be reconnected instead of abandoned. The room kept pointing at the conversation that no longer existed, so nothing it sent ever arrived, and the button that would have fixed it was hidden as soon as the room had been opened in Talk once — leaving no way back except deleting the room and losing its whole web transcript. A room already on Talk now offers "Reconnect to Talk", which checks the existing conversation before doing anything and creates a replacement only if the conversation is really gone. Pressing it on a healthy room says the room is already connected rather than making a second conversation. If the conversation still exists but Istota was removed from it, you are told to add the bot back rather than given a new room — a replacement there would leave the original conversation, its history and everyone else in it stranded. And if Nextcloud cannot be reached at all, the room is left exactly as it was.

- Istota no longer holds a Nextcloud connection open for every chat room, around the clock. It opened one waiting request per room on every cycle and let each sit on a Nextcloud worker until it timed out — on the production server that was 95,703 requests in a day, 97% of them abandoned having carried nothing, and enough of Nextcloud's worker pool tied up that requests started failing outright. Istota now reads the room listing once per cycle and asks only the rooms it says have a message newer than the one already read, so a quiet round costs that one short request rather than a held connection per room. The listing was previously read once a minute and is now read each cycle, which is the cost this trades for. Every room is still checked in full every five minutes whatever the listing said, so a room the shortcut misjudges is delayed by minutes rather than missed; anything unfamiliar in the listing, and a listing that could not be fetched at all, are both treated as "ask anyway". The trade is responsiveness: a message in a quiet room now waits for the next cycle instead of arriving on an open connection. Set `talk_poll_full_sweep_interval = 0` to check every room every cycle as before.

- A Talk poll timeout of zero is refused instead of silently switching Talk off. The setting was two different durations wearing one name — how long Nextcloud holds the request, and how long Istota waits for the whole round — so zero made every round give up before any reply could arrive. Messages never arrived, and nothing raised, logged or otherwise indicated that inbound Talk had stopped. The two durations are now separate, and a zero or negative value keeps the default and says so.

- Checking chat rooms for new messages no longer throws away the answers it just received. The bot asks Nextcloud to hold each request open for a set time, and then waited exactly that same time for a reply — but a reply to a request held that long cannot arrive any sooner than that, so on a quiet round every room was cut off at the moment it was about to answer, and its messages were discarded and fetched again a cycle later. The wait now allows for the reply's travel back. At the default of 30 seconds the gap is a fraction of the window and rarely showed; at the low values a deployment might set to ease load on Nextcloud it was the whole of it, so every round opened a connection per room and abandoned all of them, which is more work for Nextcloud rather than less.

- A failure while checking a chat room for new messages now says what went wrong. One unhelpful line covered three different upstream failures — an empty reply, an error page and a reply in the wrong format all produced identical text — so a deployment logging a hundred of them a day had nothing to go on. The line now carries the response status, its declared type and a short excerpt of what actually came back.

- The Linux test tier is usable as a gate again. It came back 60 red on a clean checkout from three unrelated causes, so a real regression would have arrived as one more line in a wall of noise. The largest was the test image installing the dependencies but not the project itself, which left 47 tests failing on a missing-module error that read as a code fault — and meant the built-in agent loop's tool process had never once started there. The image now puts the project on its interpreter's import path, the way every deployment already does. The other two were a one-shot regression guard whose own header had marked it for deletion once the code it froze changed on purpose, and a test whose premise is a file permission bit failing as root inside the container.

- A long task on the bot's own agent loop no longer forgets its persona, its rules and its skills partway through. Everything that defines the assistant — who it is, the tools it has, what it may and may not do, how to format an answer, the instructions for each skill it loaded — was sent as the first message of the conversation, so once the context filled up and older messages were replaced by a summary, all of it went with them and the task carried on without any of it. Those instructions are now sent as a system prompt, which nothing summarizes away, on every brain.

- Reading an uploaded lab report, visit summary or immunization record no longer gives the model access to the rest of the machine. The extraction step hands the document to a model with permission to read files, and that permission listed no directories at all — which the file tools read as "no restriction" rather than "nothing allowed" — so it covered every file the bot itself can open. The permission now names the uploaded document and nothing else. On a deployment using the Claude Code or tmux brain the exposure was wider than a read: those brains take their file boundary from the sandbox rather than from that list, and asking them for any tool at all hands them their full default toolset, including the ability to run commands. Extraction now builds a sandbox of its own — the same per-user filesystem isolation an ordinary task gets, with the uploaded document bound into it read-only and nothing else it does not need — so those brains are confined too. On a host where the sandbox cannot run — macOS, the standalone install, the shipped Docker stack — nothing is confined, which was already true of every other task there. Where a sandbox is expected and cannot be built, extraction now declines to run and says so rather than falling back to the old unconfined behaviour.



- Six places that call a model directly, outside the normal task path, no longer hand it the whole environment they were started with. Where that is the bot's own environment it holds the encryption key, the Nextcloud password, the mail passwords and every configured service token, none of which these calls need. They now build the same narrowed environment the bot already used for its other direct model calls. The six are the three document extractors, the nightly memory pass, shared briefing blocks, the biomarker explainer and the code review agents.

- **Upgrade note:** that narrowing carries the outbound-proxy, certificate-authority and gateway settings through with it, so a deployment reaching the model provider through a proxy or a custom endpoint keeps working. If you reach it some other way and one of these stops connecting after upgrading, name the variable in `security.passthrough_env_vars`.

- Fixed before it reached anyone: the bot's own agent loop could not run any tool on a Linux install with the sandbox switched on. The sandbox is told which directories to make visible inside it, and the Python installation was named by guessing its location from the source layout rather than asking the running program where it actually is. Where the guess was wrong the directory was skipped without a word, so the tool process had no Python to start and every task on that brain failed with a message about a closed connection that said nothing about the cause. The two now come from the same place. A failure to start also reports what the tool process wrote before dying, instead of only that the connection dropped.

- Switching off an auto-seeded module job now sticks. `!cron disable` on a name like `_module.feeds.prune` appeared to work and was undone within the hour. The module sync has an arm that revives a module job the daemon gave up on, and it could not tell that apart from a job you had switched off yourself, because nothing recorded which of the two had happened — so on a job that had already failed once, your off switch was read as the daemon's and reverted on the next tick, every time you used it. A disable you make is now recorded as yours and the arm leaves it alone, and `!cron` shows when you switched a job off the way it already showed when the scheduler suspended one. One case is deliberately not covered: switching the whole module off and back on deletes and re-seeds its jobs, so a job you had disabled comes back on — re-enabling a module is a fresh instruction rather than a revert.

- `!cron enable` and `!cron disable` on a module job no longer end with a note saying the change may not persist. Those jobs are seeded by the module and appear in nobody's schedule file, so the write to that file was always going to report nothing to update — the note fired every time and was the opposite of true.

- An auto-seeded module job that fails every time it runs now tells you. These jobs are kept out of the log channel and were also left out of the notifications, so one that never worked left no trace but a database field you had to go and read — for the nightly feeds cleanup that means nothing gets deleted, and the symptom is the unbounded growth the job exists to prevent. You now get one notification the first time the scheduler gives up on such a job, carrying the error and the command that stops the retries, and it stays in the list until the job works again or you switch it off. Only the first is sent: the scheduler keeps retrying these on its own, so each later failure updates the existing notification rather than pushing again, and dismissing it stays quiet too — the next alert about that job waits until it has worked at least once in between.

- The browser no longer freezes after a handful of page loads. Chrome was being asked to write its log into a pipe nothing read, and once that pipe filled — two or three pages' worth — the write blocked and took Chrome's main thread with it. The browser looked alive from every angle: animations kept running, its debug port kept answering, and the container reported healthy, while clicks did nothing and every page fetch hung until a 90-second timer killed the browser and started it again. Measured on the production container at 63,199 of 65,536 bytes after three renders. The log now goes nowhere unless you ask for it with `CHROME_LOG_STDERR=1`, which sends it to the container's own output instead.

- A browser restart no longer leaves dead processes behind. Chrome was killed without waiting for it, and only the browser process was signalled, so its renderer and GPU children were orphaned — 43 defunct processes accumulated in 40 minutes on the production host, against a container limit of 9,507. The whole process group is now signalled and collected, and the container runs an init to catch anything that still escapes.

- After a browser restart, a page you had open no longer reports "Tab not found" for the next ten minutes. Sessions are numbered tabs in the running browser, and the restart left them pointing at tabs that no longer existed. They are now discarded when the browser they belonged to goes away, so the next request opens a fresh one.

- A browser that has to be rescued repeatedly now reports itself unhealthy and gets restarted. The rescue timer healed the same fault every 90 seconds for hours while every health check stayed green, because a hung browser satisfies all of them. Configurable via `BROWSER_WEDGE_RECOVERY_THRESHOLD` (default 3) and `BROWSER_WEDGE_RECOVERY_WINDOW_S` (default 900).

- A JavaScript dialog on a page no longer stops the browser dead. There is no window manager behind the browser, so an `alert()`, a "leave site?" prompt or Chrome's own "page unresponsive" dialog had nothing that could close it. Dialogs are now answered as they appear, and the dialogs Chrome raises on its own are switched off.

- When the browser container answers with something that is not JSON — an internal error page, a gateway error from something in front of it, an empty reply — the browse commands now report the status code and quote the start of the body. Every one of those used to come back as `Expecting value: line 1 column 1 (char 0)`, the same string whatever had gone wrong, so the outage below could only be diagnosed by reading the container's logs.

- Browsing no longer strands a browser tab when a page fetch fails partway. `browse links --selector` opens a session, reads it, then closes it; if the read failed the close was skipped, and with only two tabs available the lost one stayed locked for ten minutes. Fetching links from a site that errors used to cost half the browser's capacity each time.

- The browse commands now report a failure by exiting non-zero. They exited 0 on every error the container itself described — bad arguments, an expired session, a page that would not load — so anything checking the exit status read a failed fetch as a success. Two consequences worth knowing: `browse render` against an expired session now exits non-zero where it used to be a soft answer, and a scheduled job running a browse command records the real message instead of `Exit code 1`. That last fix covers the feeds and money jobs too, which had been losing their error messages the same way.

- A backtick fence appearing anywhere inside your scheduled jobs list no longer cuts the list short. The bot read only as far as the first three backticks after the list opened — inside one of your own comments, say, or inside the text of a job — and where that break fell between two jobs the remainder was still a valid list, so nothing warned: it took the jobs below the break for ones you had deleted and removed them, while your file went on listing them. Nothing put them back either, because every later read broke in the same place. The opening and closing markers now count only on a line of their own. Your monitoring checks and briefings files had the same flaw with milder consequences — a check or a briefing below the break quietly stopped existing — and are fixed by the same change.

- **Upgrade note:** a jobs file that was being read short is now read whole, so any job dropped this way comes back at the next sync, out of your own file. That is the fix working rather than something to undo. Worth a look at your schedule after upgrading if you remember a job going missing without your having deleted it.

- One consequence runs the other way. A marker that shares its line with anything else — text before the opening one, a note after the closing one — no longer opens or closes the list, because that is the same ambiguity the fix exists to remove. A file like that keeps whatever schedule is already running and the bot logs that it could not read the list, rather than acting on a partial read or treating the file as empty. Putting the markers on lines of their own is the fix. Indentation, four or more backticks, Windows line endings and a stray non-breaking space after the closing marker are all still accepted, so a file that reads correctly today goes on doing so.

- A scheduled job whose text holds a backslash or a quote is no longer destroyed the next time anything rewrites your jobs file. Disabling a job, or a one-off job removing itself, re-writes every job in that file without putting back the escaping the format needs, so a prompt carrying a regular expression or a quoted phrase either broke the whole file — freezing your schedule until you repaired it by hand — or came back silently altered into a different instruction. Every field now escapes on the way out; the one visible change is that a multi-line command is written on a single line with escapes, and reads back identically.
- Web browsing no longer breaks until someone restarts the browser container. When the container ran short of memory it closed the oldest open page from a background thread, which wrecked the machinery it uses to drive the browser; every page fetch after that failed, for hours, while the browser itself stayed up and every health check stayed green. The clean-up now happens on the thread that owns that machinery, and any other thread reaching for it is refused outright instead of quietly breaking it for good.

- The browser container now notices that fault itself and gets restarted for it. Its health check could tell whether the browser was alive but not whether the container could still drive it, so the one failure that needs a restart was the one nothing detected — those hours of dead browsing ended at an unrelated scheduled restart rather than by anything spotting them. It now keeps track of whether page fetches are reaching the browser at all, reports itself unhealthy after a short run of failures, and the watchdog that already restarts wedged browser containers takes it from there. On the Docker deployment nothing acts on that verdict, which the deployment guide now says.

- On the Docker deployment, editing `docker/.env` and rebuilding now changes what the bot runs. It did not before: the settings file was written on the very first boot and never again, so every one of the 170 settings that file controls was fixed for the life of the install, and changing one produced no error, no warning and no change. The settings file is now written from `docker/.env` on every start, the boot log names every setting that changed, and the outgoing file is kept beside it as `config.toml.prev`. Values worked out once during setup — the Nextcloud login for the web UI, the chat room addresses, the location tracking token, the key that signs web sessions — are carried across rather than made again, so nobody is logged out and no phone stops reporting. If you deliberately hand-edit the settings file inside the container, set `ISTOTA_CONFIG_RENDER=preserve` to keep the old behaviour; the boot then lists every setting that has drifted from `docker/.env` instead of saying nothing.

- **Upgrade note:** the same change makes three previously outstanding Docker patches into a restart. An install created before the Nextcloud folder-prefix release, before `[models.roles]` became `[models.aliases]`, or before the tmux brain's failover became explicit picks all three up on its next start. The one thing a restart still cannot do is the Nextcloud side of the first: enabling sharing on the two external mounts, which is an `occ` call and is written out in the Docker deployment guide.

- A one-time scheduled job finishing can no longer stall the whole bot. Removing its entry from `CRON.md` happens on the Nextcloud mount, and it was done while the task still held the database open for writing, so a mount that stopped answering blocked every other part of the bot that needed to write — the job dispatcher, the other running tasks, the web interface, the pollers — until the mount timed out. The file is now written after the database work is finished and released.

- Turning off native session transcripts now stops new writes without abandoning transcripts already on disk. The configured age and disk limits keep deleting old files until both are set to zero.

- Switching the credential proxy off while the sandbox is on now warns about what that really costs. The startup message named only the visible half — skill commands lose the databases they read — and said nothing about the quiet half: every service credential you have configured stays in the task's own environment, where the model can read it from inside the boundary you just switched on. The settings reference, the example settings file and the deployment guide gave the same partial answer and now give both halves. Nothing changes for an install running both switches off, which is the single-user shape and is unaffected.

### Security

- The files the bot writes for a task — the standing instructions it runs under, the request it was given, and any images prepared for it — have moved out of the scratch directory the task itself can write into. They used to sit beside the task's own working files, so a second task of the same user running at the same time could overwrite the instructions, and any later task of that user could read the request for as long as the scratch files are kept. They now live in a directory owned by the bot that no task can write and only the task they belong to can read, so anything the framework puts there in future is covered without a new rule.

- The health check now says whether the sandbox is actually working, not just whether bubblewrap is installed. Those are different questions and the answer differs on a shipped deployment: the Docker stack installs bubblewrap and has the sandbox switched on, but the container is not granted the two settings bubblewrap needs to build the isolation, so every task there runs with the bot's own access to the machine while every health surface reported the sandbox as fine. `istota doctor`, the startup report and the admin Health pane now carry a separate line for it, carrying the fix for a container and for a bare host beside it. An install that switched the sandbox off on purpose — the single-user one does — is skipped rather than failed. The bubblewrap line is unchanged and keeps answering its own question, because a missing bubblewrap and an installed one that cannot build the isolation are separate faults with separate repairs.

- Two consequences of that worth knowing before you upgrade. It will fail on the Docker stack and on a Mac running the default config, which is what it is for — the condition is not new, only the first report of one you already had. And a failing check is alerted to admins once at every daemon start, so a Docker deployment left as shipped gets one alert per restart until it is granted the two settings, switches the sandbox off to say it is deliberately unconfined, or moves to the bare-metal install where the sandbox works. The hourly re-check does not repeat it; only a start counts.

- Running the sandbox without the credential proxy is now reported by the health check, not only in a boot log you read once at install. `istota doctor` and the admin Health pane showed that pairing as a plain "not applicable" line carrying nothing but the setting name; it is now a standing warning naming what is exposed — every service credential you have configured, sitting in the task environment where the model can read it — plus what a startup message cannot tell you: whether the sandbox you asked for is actually working on this host, or whether it failed and the task is running unconfined as well. An install with both switches off is reported as the deliberate choice it is.

- Shell commands run by the bot's own agent loop no longer land in a sandbox with the Claude Code login file mounted into it. That sandbox was built for the Claude Code CLI and carried everything the CLI needs, the stored login among it — read-only, which stops it being changed but not read, so a task could simply print the file and the text went off to whichever model provider the loop is pointed at. There are now two sandboxes: the CLI keeps the one it needs, and the bot's own loop gets one with no Claude installation mounted in — no login file, no settings, no session logs. Only affects a Linux install with the sandbox working; nothing changes for the Claude Code path.
