- The bot's own agent loop now reads and writes files inside the sandbox rather than around it. Its file tools — read, write, edit, search and file listing — ran in the bot's own process, kept inside your workspace by a list of allowed paths written in Python; only shell commands were sandboxed, and those got a fresh sandbox for every command. All six now run in one sandbox per task, so a path outside your workspace is not there to be reached rather than being refused by a check. That closes a gap the list could never close: a task could swap a folder along a path between the moment the path was checked and the moment the file was opened, and be handed a file from elsewhere on the machine. The allowed-path list is still applied, and is what produces the clear "outside your workspace" message instead of a bare "no such file".

- Two side effects of that, both visible. Everything a shell command starts is now inside the task's memory and process limits, including anything it starts in the background — before, the parts that did the real work escaped those limits on every sandboxed command. And a long-running task can be stopped again: the bot records the process it can signal, which it had never done for its own loop, so `!stop` and the web Stop button had nothing to reach for.

- One behaviour change worth knowing: `/tmp` and anything a command leaves running in the background now survive from one command to the next within a task, instead of being thrown away with each command's sandbox. That matches how the Claude Code path has always behaved. Nothing changes on a machine without the sandbox — a Mac, the single-user install, or the Docker stack without the two container settings — where the commands run exactly as they did.

- A task running on the native brain could read the bot's Claude subscription token out of its own shell environment, and anything a task reads becomes text sent to the model. The native brain talks to whichever provider you configured it for, which on most setups is not the company that issued that token. The token is set for every task whatever brain will run it, because the two Claude brains sign in with it; it is now taken back out both on the way into the native brain's tools and on the way into the skills the bot runs on your behalf, neither of which ever read it. Deployments that keep the token in the service environment and run the native brain were affected; one authenticating the Claude CLI by its credentials file alone was not.

- The files in your workspace's `config/` folder — your memory file, your persona, your scheduled jobs, your monitoring checks, your task list, and the examples the bot keeps up to date there — are read and written by the bot itself, outside the sandbox, where it can see far more of the machine than a task can. A task can write to that folder; that is the feature. What it could also do was put a link there in place of one of those files, or in place of the folder, and the bot followed it: a link at your memory file put the contents of some other file on the machine into the next task's prompt, and a link at the folder turned a memory write into a file of the task's choosing written wherever it liked. Every read and write of that folder now refuses to follow a link out of your own workspace, and refuses anything that is not an ordinary file. A room's shared notes file and your daily memory files get the same treatment, for the same reason.

- The same change closes two ways to jam the bot indefinitely. A named pipe left at one of those paths made the read wait for a writer that never came. For the memory file and the persona that read happens while a task's prompt is being assembled — before there is a task to time out — so one such file stopped every later task for that person, with nothing logged to say why. For the scheduled jobs and the monitoring checks it was worse: those are read on the bot's own timer, so a single pipe stopped scheduled work for everyone, not just the person whose folder it was in. Those reads now refuse a pipe instead of waiting on it. The per-skill customization files were already protected this way; this brings the rest of the folder in line with them, using the same code rather than a second copy of it.

- The nightly memory tidy-up will no longer overwrite a memory file it could not read. It treated "could not read this" and "this is empty" as the same answer, so an unreadable memory file looked like a blank one, the safety check that compares before and after compared one unreadable read against another and saw no difference, and the night's edits were written over the top. A memory file grown past the size the bot will read — which a task can do to its own — was erased that way, and the run reported success. It now stops and says so, and that size limit is raised well past any real memory file.

- Everything that reads your per-skill customization files now holds that folder open while it reads, instead of looking the path up again for each file. The folder lives inside the workspace a task can write to, so a task could rename it and put a link in its place between the check and the read, and be handed a file from somewhere else entirely. The worst case was the prompt itself: up to 32 KB of any file the bot can read, appended to a skill's instructions on every task that used the skill. The same change covers `skills overlay`, `skills overlays`, `istota doctor` and the nightly memory pass; the search index was fixed earlier.

- One consequence you may see: if you deliberately made `config/skills` a link to somewhere else in your own workspace, its customizations no longer load. Following a link is what the above closes, and a link that stays inside your own folder cannot be told from one that has just been repointed. `istota doctor` reports the folder by name with what to do about it, so this shows up as a finding rather than as customizations quietly going missing.

- The directory where coding work happens is now split per person. It was one tree, and every admin's task was handed all of it to read and write — so any admin could read or change any other admin's clones, working copies, git settings and downloaded packages. Each admin now gets their own subtree and nothing above it, which removes the sharing rather than guarding it. Both exposures below were admin-to-admin, so an install with a single admin was never at risk; that is not a property anyone was maintaining on purpose.

- Two open problems close with it. The download cache lived in that shared tree, so every admin's task could read and change every other admin's, and those caches hold unpacked packages that the next install trusts and runs. And the code-review command, which takes a path to a working copy, checked that path against the whole shared tree — it runs outside the sandbox, so the check was the only thing standing between one admin's task and another's checkout. The cache is now inside your own subtree and the path check is against it, refusing rather than falling back to the shared tree when it cannot tell whose task is asking.

- Where that cache goes is now worked out rather than configured. It has to sit inside the directory a task is handed, because that is what lets an install share files with it instead of copying every package, and the bot derives the path from your own subtree instead of taking it from a setting. The setting is still there for an install that runs the sandbox without the coding feature, where there is no repository tree to put a cache in; a deploy leaves it unset otherwise, and the per-user size limit and the periodic sweep that enforces it are unchanged.

- **Upgrade note:** a deploy moves an existing repository directory into the new shape for you, one project namespace at a time, repairing each working copy as it goes. It needs exactly one admin configured to know who owns what, and refuses without touching anything if there are none or several. If a task happens to be running it also does nothing, but the deploy carries on and says so at the end rather than failing: that refusal clears by itself once the task finishes, and failing the deploy never stopped the new code reaching the machine anyway. Re-run the deploy when the bot is idle. Afterwards a `.package-caches` directory is left at the top of the tree; nothing reads it any more and you can remove it once you have looked at what is in it.

- A question that cache change left open turned out to be a real hole, and is now closed. One of a person's tasks can swap their download cache for a link to somewhere else while a second of their tasks is starting, and the system does follow it — measured on a real kernel, handing the second task read-write access to whatever the link pointed at. For an admin that was already harmless, because the directory they are handed gets attached over the top of it a moment later. For a **non-admin** it was not: the cache was being placed inside their coding directory whether or not they were an admin, while the thing that covers it is admin-only, and the dev container mounts that same directory writable for anyone an operator lists. The cache is now placed there only for the people who get the covering directory, and falls back to the ordinary location otherwise. The belief about which setting had been preventing this turned out to be wrong as well, which is why the gap was not noticed.

- **Upgrade note:** on an install with the coding feature on, a non-admin now gets no on-disk download cache unless `security.sandbox_cache_dir` is set — package downloads go back to memory for them, as before that cache existed. Admins are unaffected. Nobody who was not already an admin could do coding work in that directory, so nothing they could use is lost.

- Tasks in the Docker deployment ran with no sandbox around them. Bubblewrap only puts itself in a user namespace unasked when it is not running as root, and the container runs as root — so the check that asks "can bubblewrap work here" failed, the sandbox was switched off for the whole process, and every task since had the framework database, every user's module databases and the rendered config file in view. The check now asks the follow-up question, and where the answer is yes the flag is spelled out on every sandbox the bot builds. The bare-metal deployment runs as an ordinary user and is unaffected; a bare-metal install running as root with a restricted capability set would have had the same problem and is fixed by the same change.

- That check also asks a bigger question than it used to. It confirmed only that the system would grant a private view of the filesystem, while the sandbox the bot then builds does more than that — and a container can allow the first and refuse the rest, which would have had the bot report a working sandbox and then fail every task. It now performs the same operations the real sandbox does, so its answer is about the thing it is asked to vouch for.

- Running the sandbox under Docker needs two container settings the shipped compose file deliberately does not grant, so a Docker deployment stays unsandboxed until an operator adds them — and it now says so honestly rather than starting and failing. Both are recorded, with what was measured, in `docker/docker-compose.test.yml`; the supported production shape is bare metal, where neither is needed.
- Updating the source on a server now restarts the two per-user proxies as well, so a fix to either actually takes effect. They were left out of the restart list on both the deploy and the two-minute auto-update, and their own restarts fired only when the deployment changed shape — so between two such changes, a fix to the Docker allowlist or the credential proxy landed on disk and the old code kept running indefinitely. That is why the keep-alive fix above was still bypassable on a server that had it.

- The dev container's network rules are now put at the front of the firewall chain they live in rather than appended to the end of it. A chain stops at its first pass-or-block decision, and Docker before v28 ships that chain with one already in it — so on those hosts all four rules were listed by the firewall and never consulted, and the container had none of the network isolation the bot's own prompt told it it had. A deploy now also repairs a host whose rules are already in the wrong place, which the previous check-before-adding approach could not do: it asked whether the rule was in the chain, and on a broken host the answer was yes. `istota doctor` grew a check that reads the live chain and reports anything sitting in front of those rules, because a rule set nothing evaluates looks identical to a working one; reading the chain needs root, so it says plainly when it could not look rather than passing quietly.

- The browser's remote-view console no longer publishes on every network interface by default, on either the Ansible or the Docker install, and can no longer be published without a password. It serves an interactive view of the browser holding your logged-in profile, and it was reachable on all interfaces with no password set — while the browser's own API port, configured on the line directly above it, was correctly restricted to the machine itself. The address is now a setting rather than a fixed value, defaulting to the machine itself, and an Ansible deploy refuses outright if you point it somewhere reachable without setting a password, or if you set an external URL it could never resolve to.

- **Upgrade note:** if you reach that console over a VPN, set `istota_browser_vnc_bind_address` to the address you reach it on before deploying, or it will stop being reachable. The default change is what closes the hole for everyone who never knew the console was open; keeping your own access is one line of inventory.

- The dev container's blocklist covers several ranges it previously left open. It blocked one cloud-metadata address; it now blocks the whole link-local range, which is where AWS also serves instance DNS, NTP, and the endpoint that hands out container role credentials. Added Azure's host-agent address, which is neither link-local nor private and so was matched by nothing, and the carrier-grade NAT range that Tailscale uses — on a host that joins a tailnet, the container would otherwise have reached straight into it. A rule the old version installed and this one supersedes is now removed on upgrade rather than left behind forever.

- Turning on IPv6 for the dev container's network would silently disable all of this, since every rule is IPv4-only, and a test now fails if anyone does. That is deliberately a guard rather than a fix: writing the IPv6 rules is the real answer, and this makes it impossible to open the gap without first noticing it exists.

- Two things change on a host still running Docker 27 or earlier, where these rules were inert until now. Check your upstream DNS resolver's address first — on those versions the container's own lookups cross these rules, and a resolver on a private address is one of the things they block. The dev container also loses reach to other containers on private Docker networks, which is the isolation working as intended but shows up as "it cannot reach anything" rather than as a DNS error.

- The guard that runs before every commit the bot makes now refuses a commit it could not fully check. Of the two scans there, the one that recognises a credential by its shape was never installed on the server, so every commit the bot had ever made passed on the pattern list alone. It is now installed as part of a deploy, pinned to a version that works, and a commit nobody is watching is refused rather than half-checked; someone committing from their own machine still gets a warning and the commit, because refusing them is how a guard ends up switched off for good.

- The Docker allowlist that guards the bot's escape-hatch container now checks every request on a connection, not just the first. A program inside a task could open a connection with one harmless request — the handshake the Docker client itself sends — and then put anything it liked down the same connection, and everything after the first was copied through unread; a container really was created this way in testing, and the same route would have carried one with the host's disk attached. Each request is now read and checked on its own, a request the allowlist and the Docker service could read differently is refused rather than guessed at, and the audit log records every request rather than only the first.

- The Go toolchain inside the dev container is now checked against a known hash before it is unpacked, as the other tools in that image already were. It was the one download in the image that was taken on trust.

- A sender can no longer compose the version of their email that you read. The bot wraps an incoming message in tags that mark it as text from outside, and the chat transcript strips those before showing it to you. A subject line or an attachment name can legally carry a line break, and one carrying the right text could close the wrapper early and open a replacement — so the transcript would show a message the sender had written in full, headers and all, under the real sender's name, with the actual message never displayed. Those values are now flattened to a single line before the wrapper is built, the sender shown is the first one given rather than the last, and a message body that tries the same trick now displays the attempt instead of hiding what follows it. This was only reachable once the stripping started working, which is a fix in the same release.

- A task can no longer rewrite the helper scripts that fetch its own credentials. Those scripts are written into a `.developer` directory inside each task's scratch space, and the sandbox has always kept them read-only — but the in-process agent, which runs without the sandbox, treated the whole scratch space as writable. A task could therefore replace the script that asks for a forge token with one that did something else with it. That directory is now excluded from what a task may write on both paths.

- The token the server uses to fetch its own updates is no longer written into the repository's git config or passed on a command line. It was stored there when the server was first set up, which also put it in the argument list of every update check — one every two minutes — where anyone with root on the box could read it in passing. A deploy now keeps it in a root-only file that git asks for it when needed, and rewrites the old value out of servers already set up this way; rotate the token afterwards, since anything provisioned before this had it sitting in a file and in process arguments.

- A credential left inside a repository's git settings is now removed before the bot can read it. Coding work happens in clones under a directory the bot reads and writes, and ordinary git commands print those settings back in full — so a token stored in one reaches the bot's context as a matter of routine, and from there a task result, a transcript and the memory index. It covers the places a token actually hides, not just the obvious one: the repository address, an address pulled in from another file, a rewrite rule that carries the secret in its own name, a per-worktree override, and an authorization header.

- Anything removed that way is named in the log so you know which credential to rotate — treat it as disclosed, since it was readable for as long as it sat there. Repositories on the forge you have configured keep working, because git asks for that token separately; a repository on some other host will need its own credential helper afterwards. Where a credential is found but cannot be removed — a config being written by another task, a read-only disk — that is logged too, rather than passing as clean.

- Quoting a message the bot sent no longer gets your agent to run on the strength of one header. Any such mail was processed without asking you, on the reasoning that only the recipient would hold the identifier linking it to the thread — but that identifier travels to everyone copied in, to everyone the thread is forwarded to, and into any archive the thread reaches, and it never expired. Replies from the address the bot wrote to are unaffected; anyone else on the thread now waits for your approval, and `yes trust` lets them through for good.

- The check that watches your inbound mail path can now tell your mail server's own verdict from one a sender wrote. It read the top authentication header on the assumption that your server had put it there — true while your server is stamping, and wrong in the one case the check exists to catch, because if the stamping stops the sender's own header is the top one and a forged pass reads as a healthy path. Set `authserv_id` to the name your mail server stamps and everything else is discarded; mail arriving without your server's stamp then raises a warning on its own. The check also reports the DKIM and SPF results alongside, and warns when a pass turns out to be about a different address than the one the mail claimed to come from — that last one applies whether or not you set `authserv_id`, so an existing install may start seeing it without changing anything.

- The confirmation gate for mail claiming your own address can now ask your mail server instead of asking you. It was all-or-nothing before: either your address was taken as proof, or every message you sent the bot was held for approval — and the second is unusable, because nothing in a plain email distinguishes you from someone imitating you, so it had to interrupt you every time. Set `confirm_sender_match = "verify"` and mail that authenticates cleanly goes straight through, with the question reserved for mail that does not, which on a working setup is close to never. It needs `authserv_id` set and refuses to start without it, since otherwise the verdict it trusts is one the sender could have written. Existing `true`/`false` settings keep working unchanged.

- A stranger's name or subject line can no longer forge formatting or extra lines in the alerts you get about their mail. Three of those alerts — the warning that your mail path stopped authenticating, the summary when someone floods you, and the question asking whether to run a message from a sender you don't know — quoted the sender and subject straight into a channel that renders formatting. A forged `From:` reading `[click here](http://somewhere-else)` arrived as a live link in a message the bot appeared to have written, and a subject can legally carry a line break, so one could also add its own `Task:` line to the question asking you to approve it. Link, code and table syntax is now stripped before sending, and the sender and subject are collapsed to one line each. A plain `http://…` address written into a name is still turned into a link by the chat client, as it is anywhere else — the bell entries beside these alerts have always worked the same way.

- The Docker socket has left the bot's sandbox entirely, and the filtering service that stood in front of it is gone with it. Every task on a deployment with a dev container could reach that socket — including tasks built from an email, a feed or a fetched page — and although what it permitted was narrow, it still allowed running commands in the container, copying files in and out, and restarting it. Nothing in a task needs it any more: coding work reaches the container over a channel the bot itself opens, and that channel is only attached to a task already trusted with the package registries. The `docker` command is no longer inside the sandbox either, so a task that reaches for it gets a missing program rather than a working one. A deploy stops and removes the per-user filtering service on every machine that ever ran one.

### Added

- **Feeds no longer keep every entry they have ever fetched.** A read entry is deleted 90 days after it arrived in your reader, and each feed is trimmed to 5000 stored entries; both numbers are on the feeds settings page, where `0` turns either limit off independently. The clock counts from when an entry was added here, not from when it was published, so an old post added to a channel today gets its full window rather than being deleted on arrival.

- The cleanup never deletes a starred entry. The 90-day rule never deletes an unread one either, nor anything the feed's most recent response still returned, and it keeps at least 50 entries per feed whatever their age — or the maximum, when you set that lower. The per-feed maximum is a hard ceiling and reads neither of those things, so on a feed already at its limit an old unread entry can go ahead of a newer read one.

- One consequence worth knowing before you rely on the 90 days: the window runs from when an entry arrived, not from when you read it. Marking a long backlog as read therefore makes everything in it that is already older than the window eligible at the next nightly run, rather than 90 days from now.

- Nothing is deleted from an existing database for the first 90 days after the upgrade. That is a deliberate safety period, not the default window in disguise: it is time to see the two numbers on the settings page and change them before anything goes. The daily cleanup runs from day one and reports the date it is waiting for. `istota-skill feeds prune --dry-run` counts what a real run would delete without touching anything, but during the safety period there is no real run to model, so it reports zeros until the period ends.

- Deleting entries does not make the database file smaller. SQLite keeps the freed pages and reuses them for later writes, so growth stops rather than reverses and a backup snapshot may still be the size it was. Nothing is vacuumed on a schedule: rewriting the file needs a lock that would fight the online backup.

- **You can give yourself a profile picture in the web UI.** Settings → Identity takes one — drop it, paste it or pick a file — and it replaces the lettered square beside your messages in chat. It is saved as soon as you choose it, with no Save step, and Remove takes it away again. The picture is resized and re-encoded on the way in, so what is stored carries none of the camera and location data a photo arrives with. It is separate from your Nextcloud picture: setting one here does not change what Nextcloud shows.

- **A profile picture you have set in Nextcloud is imported for you.** It shows up in web chat within a few hours of the bot first seeing your account, and it sits behind anything you upload here — so removing your own picture reveals it rather than leaving a lettered square. Only a picture you actually set is taken: Nextcloud draws a coloured letter for an account that has none, and importing that would replace one lettered square with another. Operators can switch the import off with `[web] avatar_import_from_nextcloud`, and `istota doctor` reports what the last pass did.

- **Other people's pictures show up too.** A message written by someone else in a shared room now carries their picture rather than a lettered square, and the users table on `/admin` shows a face beside each name. You see the picture of anyone you share a room with; a message mirrored in from outside — an email from a contact — keeps its lettered square, since the sender has no account here.

- **An admin can give the bot a picture of its own.** The Bot icon card at the foot of `/admin` takes one the same way Settings takes yours, and it replaces the amber lettered square wherever the web UI names the bot — including the sign-in page, which shows it in place of the octopus. It applies to everyone on the deployment. It is not the picture Nextcloud shows for the bot's account and cannot change it, so set that one in Nextcloud if you want them to match. For a scripted install there is `istota bot-icon set <path>`, plus `clear` and `show`; setting the same file twice does nothing, so a deploy can run it every time.

- **You can pinch to zoom an image opened full screen, in the app and in a browser.** Drag to pan once it is zoomed in, double-tap to jump in and back out, and on a laptop pinch the trackpad or hold Ctrl and scroll. A screenshot or a chart in a feed post used to open at whatever size fitted the screen with no way to magnify it, which on a phone is the case where opening it full screen was the point. Tapping the image still closes it, and the keyboard now zooms too: `+`, `-` and `0`.

- **You can type and send in web chat while the bot is still working on a reply.** The message is queued instead of refused: it appears in the transcript straight away, dimmed and marked "Waiting to send", and goes out as the next turn when the current one finishes and you are still in the room. Your message is not sent until then, so a queued one is still yours to edit back into the box or remove. Turns here run for minutes, and holding your next thought for the whole of one was the cost this removes.

- Stop is now its own button, to the left of a Send button that is always Send. One control that changed meaning as a turn started could be tapped for the meaning it no longer had — a tap aimed at Send arriving as a cancel — and two buttons cannot do that. Stop appearing and disappearing never moves Send, so a tap lands where it was aimed whatever the turn does mid-gesture. It takes the place of the voice-message button rather than sitting beside it, which keeps the message box the same width and stops it growing to two rows for the length of every turn; you can still queue a typed message, and the microphone comes back when the turn ends.

- A queued message goes out only when the turn it was written behind ends normally. Press Stop, hit a failure, or leave a turn waiting on a confirmation, and everything behind it is held rather than fired at work you just abandoned; each held message gets a Send button of its own. Queued messages survive a reload, and one that was waiting behind a turn comes back held, because a page load must never send anything by itself. Ten per room is the limit, and the composer says so rather than swallowing the eleventh.

- **The recent messages of every room you have opened are kept on the device, so losing the connection no longer empties web chat.** The transcript you were reading stays on screen, switching rooms still shows what was last said in each, and a row above the composer says the app cannot reach your server rather than leaving you to work it out from things not loading. About fifty messages per room and the twenty rooms you looked at most recently, expiring after a month. A room with nothing saved says so instead of inviting you to ask a question that would only wait; the All, Unread and Starred views are empty offline, since they are a question only the server can answer. Everything comes back the moment your server is reachable, without a reload.

- **The iOS app now opens with no connection at all, where before it showed the "cannot connect to the server" page.** It needs the app build that ships alongside this and one launch with a connection first, after which a cold start in airplane mode boots into your room list, the last room you were reading, and a composer that queues. This is the piece the rest of the offline work was waiting on: nothing saved on the phone was reachable while the app could not get as far as running. In a desktop browser nothing changes.

- Settings has a "Clear offline data" row, in the app only. It drops the saved copy of the app and everything kept for reading offline, and the app fetches both again — the way out of a phone that has got hold of a bad copy of something, where a reload cannot help because the reload is served from that copy. Messages waiting to send are kept; a file held with one is not.

- **A message you write in web chat with no connection is kept and sent when the connection comes back.** It appears in the transcript straight away, dimmed and marked "Waiting for a connection", with the same Edit and Remove a message waiting behind a running turn gets, and it goes out by itself once your server is reachable again — in the order you wrote it, after the app has caught up on whatever it missed. No request is attempted in the meantime, so there is no half-minute wait to be told what the offline row above the composer already says. Messages waiting in a room you are not looking at show as a count beside that room's name in the list; open the room and they go.

- A send that was already on its way when the connection dropped is kept the same way rather than reported as failed. The server was never reached, so nothing refused the message and calling it failed was a verdict nobody had given. It cannot be sent twice either: every message carries an identity the server recognises, so a second attempt is answered with the first one's reply rather than starting a second one. That covers the ambiguous case the old behaviour got wrong in the other direction — force-quitting the app between sending and the reply, where the message used to disappear from the phone while the server may well have had it.

- **A voice note recorded with no connection is kept too, and uploaded and transcribed when the connection comes back.** The same holds for a photo or any other file you attach: its chip appears in the composer dimmed rather than failing, the message waits with it, and the file goes up first when your server is reachable again — after which the message follows, referring to it. Nothing about recording changes; the memo simply follows whichever path is open. A file that goes up and a connection that drops again before the message does are not repeated work: what already reached the server stays there and the next attempt picks up where it stopped.

- A file too large to keep on the phone is refused as you attach it, with a sentence saying to attach it when you are back online, rather than being taken and then filling up the space the app has to work in — which the messages waiting to send and the conversations kept for reading offline share. Roughly ten megabytes for one file and fifty across everything waiting; a voice note is a small fraction of the first. A file the server itself turns away when it finally goes up fails that message with the server's own reason and offers no retry, since retrying cannot change the answer; what you wrote stays on screen.

- Anything still waiting when you close the app is there when you open it again, and sends itself if it was written in the last day. Older than that it waits for you to press Send: a message written days ago arrives in a conversation that has moved on, and firing it while you are looking at something else is the surprise worth avoiding.

- **A skill's instructions can now be customized per person, without forking the skill.** Give a skill a markdown file of your own in your bot's config folder and its contents are appended to that skill's instructions whenever the skill is in use, with a line saying your text wins where the two disagree. It is an addition, not a replacement: edits to the shipped skill keep arriving underneath it. The override that already existed swaps out the whole document, which meant hand-merging every upstream change forever to add three lines.

- This is where a rule belongs when it only matters while one skill is in use — a filing convention for notes, a routing rule for the calendar, how long a test run takes on your machine. Those rules had nowhere to live but your main memory file, which is read on every task including the ones that will never touch a note or a calendar. The bot is told the test for choosing: a rule it would be *wrong* to ignore on a task where the skill did not load stays in the main file, because skill selection is a guess and a prohibition needs to be loudest on the task that did not recognize what it was.

- A whole subsection of a memory file can now be removed, which nothing could do before. Removing text was limited to single bullets and to whole top-level sections, so a subsection built out of prose, numbered steps or bold sub-headers could not be removed at all through the bot's own tools — the only way to tidy one up was to edit the file by hand, which skips the locking and the audit trail every other write goes through. `remove-subheading` drops one, on a memory file or a per-skill overlay.

- Removing the last bullet under a subheading now removes the subheading too. It used to be left behind, announcing a section with nothing in it, and on a per-skill overlay that empty heading was then loaded into the prompt of every task using the skill. This is the same rule that already deleted an overlay file once its last line went.

- Write and inspect them through the memory commands you already use, so they get the same lock, the same atomic write and the same audit trail: the existing append, show, remove and replace verbs take a new flag naming the skill, and one more lists what is customized and whether each file actually loads. Never edit them by hand. They are also indexed for search, and the nightly memory tidy-up is shown their names so it does not copy a rule back into your main file a week after it moved out.

- Two guards worth knowing about. The safety instructions accept no addition of this kind, and a file the size of a whole skill document is not loaded at all — that size means somebody pasted a forked skill into the wrong place, which would otherwise put two contradictory sets of instructions in one prompt with no error anywhere. A file named for a skill that does not exist is reported by the inventory command and by `istota doctor`, since it looks configured and is read by nothing.

- The setting that moves a task's package downloads out of memory and onto disk now has something that prunes what it leaves behind. It runs on a timer and gives each user's cache the package managers' own cleanup first, which keeps the entries still in use; a cache still over its size limit afterwards is emptied. Nothing is deleted by hand, and a user with a task running is skipped rather than raced. The setting itself is still off by default while where the directory should live is settled.

- The bot's coding work can now build and test inside the dev container instead of on the host. Package installs and test runs used to happen in the same locked-down space the agent itself runs in, which cannot reach a package registry that was not listed in advance and cannot install a system package — so an ordinary `npm ci` or `uv sync` failed in a way that read as a flaky network. Switch it on per deployment and those commands run in that user's container instead, with their real exit codes, their full output and no timeout of their own, while the files they produce stay where the bot can read and edit them. Off by default and unchanged for anyone who does not turn it on.

- **Upgrade note for everyone with coding work enabled, whether or not you turn this on:** the directory holding clones and worktrees gains a level, one folder per user, and existing clones move down into it. That part is not optional and is not about containers — it is what stops one person's coding task reaching another's checkouts. A deploy moves them for you where there is only one user configured; with several it cannot work out who owns what, because the old layout recorded no owner, so it reports what it would move, changes nothing and stops the deploy until you name the user. It refuses to move anything holding uncommitted work, or anything written to in the last quarter of an hour.

- **And for anyone turning the container on:** the dev container image is rebuilt so that it and the bot share one identity on disk. Without that, either the container cannot write into a checkout or the bot can never tidy one up afterwards, and neither failure says why. Every dev container is also recreated with a different program at its heart — it used to sit idle waiting to be shelled into, and now it runs the service the bot talks to. Its home volume repairs its own ownership on the first start after the rebuild, once, so the first boot is slower and no later one is; watch the container's log for that line and for the service settling rather than restarting in a loop. `istota doctor` grew four checks that report all of this before a task hits it.

- A deploy can now be told to rebuild the dev container and recreate it from scratch, with `-e istota_devbox_force_recreate=true`. It normally rebuilds on its own when the container's definition or any file inside it changes, which covers the ordinary case; this is for the case it cannot see, such as a container someone changed by hand or an image left half-built by a failed run. It reuses cached build layers, so a stale base image still needs one build by hand.

- A bell in the app bar now shows what is waiting on you, from every page rather than only from the chat. Two things could be held for your answer — an email from an unknown sender, and a reply the bot drafted but has not sent — and both were only visible if you happened to open the chat, so a message held while you were on the money pages sat there until you did. Clicking the bell lists what is open, with the same Confirm and Discard buttons as before, and a "Needs action" filter for the items that want a decision rather than a look.

- An answer given anywhere closes the item everywhere. Replying "yes" in your chat room, approving from email, or letting the question time out all clear it from the list, and the list re-checks each item as it opens, so a question answered on another surface can never sit there looking unanswered. Items you have already been told about are deduplicated rather than repeated — a nightly job failing four nights running is one entry marked four times, not four entries.

- Three things that used to fail in silence now appear in the bell. A scheduled job switched off after failing five times running told nobody at all — it just stopped producing, and the first sign was a briefing that never arrived. A Garmin connection whose stored login stopped working flipped the settings card to "not connected" and pushed nothing, and because the sync job removes itself once the login is gone, that failure was also the last one that would ever happen. And lab results uploaded but never confirmed stay out of the dashboard and out of the trend charts, so an upload abandoned halfway through was data you could no longer see. Each now leaves an entry that says what happened and closes itself when you fix it — the job runs again, the connection is re-made, the results are confirmed.

- Warnings that used to be sent once and then vanish now leave an entry in the bell too. A security concern the bot raised while reading a message, a request you emailed in that timed out before you answered it, an answer that could not be delivered, a notice that mail was filed unread or held waiting for your approval, and a warning that mail claiming to come from your own address failed its authentication check were each a single push and nothing behind it — so if you had no alert destination set up, or the send simply failed, the warning was gone and in one case the evidence was deleted with it. Each now leaves an entry carrying the detail, and clears itself once you have read it. Repeats collapse rather than pile up: fifty forged messages in one burst leave a single entry, counted fifty times and naming the senders, instead of fifty separate things to clear. Entries nobody reads are cleared after two weeks, so the count cannot climb forever.

- The two floating strips at the top of the chat pane are gone with it. The cards inside a conversation are unchanged: a question the bot asked mid-turn still appears in that turn, and a drafted email still appears under the reply that composed it. Anything held that has no place in a transcript — mail from a stranger, a draft from a scheduled job — is now in the bell instead of stacked above the messages. Held items you already had are carried over, so nothing waiting when you upgrade is lost.

- A room's memory is now readable and editable from the web chat. Every room keeps a `CHANNEL.md` that the bot reads at the start of every message there, and it shaped every reply while being effectively write-only from your side: you could ask the bot to remember something, but not read back what it wrote, correct a wrong entry, or set a room up with context before using it. Room settings now has an "Edit room memory" button that opens the file for editing. A save is refused while the room has a task running, and if the bot changed the file while you were typing the save stops and offers to reload rather than overwriting its work. A shared room says so — everyone in it reads and writes the same memory. Your own `USER.md` has the same gap and is unchanged for now.

- `istota doctor` checks the machine the bot is actually running on. A whole class of failure was invisible to the test suite by construction — the tests assert against Python objects on a developer's laptop, while production is a built image, a rendered config file, a `PATH` and a sandbox. The forge CLI breaking three times running was all one shape: the code assumed something about its runtime that was not true. Each of those assumptions is now written down as a check that says what it observed and what to do about it.

- The same checks run on their own. Once when the daemon starts and once an hour after that, because the drift that actually bit us happens after boot: the auto-update job pulls new code every two minutes without rewriting the config, so what is installed changes underneath a config the daemon already read. A failure sends one message to the admins rather than waiting in a log nobody opens, and only when it first appears, not every hour it persists. A failing check never stops the daemon — both deployment shapes restart automatically, so exiting would mean a crash loop that locks you out of the box you need to get into.
- The bot now stops starting new tasks when the host is short on memory, rather than adding to the pile until the machine stops answering. Tasks already running are left alone and queued ones wait for a later check, so nothing is cancelled or lost; the log gets a record naming which mounts, containers and processes hold the memory, and the admin channel gets one message per cooldown saying whether the queue is actually being held. A day of measurement on the production host also showed occasional bursts of about 1.5 GB of shared memory that no filesystem accounts for — those now produce the same record but deliberately leave the queue running, because swap absorbs them without the machine noticing, and refusing work there would cause the stall it is meant to prevent.

- A task that runs away with memory now fails on its own instead of taking the machine down with it. Each task's work — a build, a test suite, a package install — gets its own memory and process budget, so overrunning it ends that one task and leaves everything else running; before, the shortfall was the whole host's and the casualty was whichever unrelated thing the kernel happened to pick. The budget covers the whole tree a task builds, not just the process the bot starts directly, which is where the work actually happens; the experimental terminal-session backend is the exception and bounds only its own top-level process. A task stopped this way says so in the log, naming the limit it hit, rather than reporting an unexplained crash. Stray processes that outlive a task are cleaned up with it, including the ones that used to survive being told to stop. It relies on deployment settings shipped alongside it; on a server that has not taken them the bot checks at startup and reports plainly that it is running without the protection, rather than assuming it has it.

- You can now see what the bot is spending. Token counts and cost were reported by neither of the brains that run in production — one because the tool it wraps was assumed not to report them, which stopped being true a while ago, and the other because its figures went into a log line that was deleted a week later. `istota usage` reports the last 30 days and groups by day, user, model, source, brain or where the call came from; the admin dashboard shows the same figures per user beside their task counts, plus a fleet-wide pane. The record is kept for six months, well past the week tasks themselves survive.

- You can now see how much of the Claude Code plan is left. On a subscription the cost column is blank on purpose — pricing plan tokens at list rates would be inventing an invoice — so the real budget is the 5-hour and weekly rate-limit windows, which the deployment used to learn about only at the moment a task ran out of headroom and failed over. They appear as tiles on the admin dashboard, as a check in `istota doctor` and the Health pane, and in `!usage`, which comes back as a command reporting your own token totals to anyone and the fleet's plus the plan windows to an admin; the whole deployment shares one reading, and the credential behind it is read but never written or refreshed.

- Usage now covers the work the bot does on its own, not just what you ask it. The nightly memory pass, briefing sections, reading an uploaded document, and the code reviewer all call the model without a task behind them, and together they are a real share of a month's spend — they were invisible in both directions before, absent from any total and absent from any count of what was missing. Each is labelled by where it came from, so a surprising month can be traced to the thing that caused it.

- A currency figure is shown only where it is real money. On a subscription the number the tool reports is what the same usage would have cost at list price, and on some setups it is a catalog estimate that prices an unknown model at zero — both read as spend at a glance. Those show a dash instead, and are never added to a real figure; usage is read through its token counts there. Tasks that report nothing at all are counted and named rather than averaged in as zero.

- Code the bot writes for you is now reviewed before it asks you to merge it, unless the change is small enough to be a one-or-two-file edit. It used to open a merge request on the strength of its own tests, which catch what they were written to catch and nothing else; a reviewer now reads the branch diff against what the change was meant to do, and the bot fixes what comes back before pushing, reporting anything it disagreed with as a decision rather than dropping it quietly. Large changes and anything touching credentials, money or migrations get a second reviewer; where review cannot run at all the merge request still opens and says it is unreviewed; `[developer.review] enabled = false` turns it off.

- A payment that lands in your bank feed now closes the invoice it paid. The money sync booked the income and left the invoice open, so every payment needed a manual `invoice paid` afterwards. A credit that fits exactly one open invoice — same amount, issued no later than the payment — marks it paid without posting the income twice. Anything ambiguous is reported for you to settle rather than guessed at: two invoices at the same amount, or two payments fitting one invoice, are left alone and named in the output. `--tolerance` allows for a wire fee, `--no-match-invoices` turns it off, and `invoice unpaid` undoes a match that was wrong.

- A ceiling on how much inbound mail becomes work. The bot's mail address is public by design — it is the sender on every message it writes for you — so anyone who has ever had a reply from it can turn one email into a task on your account, and nothing limited how many. There is now a budget per hour, with a tighter one per correspondent so a single noisy sender throttles alone. Mail over the budget is filed rather than discarded: it stays in the mailbox, creates no task, and you get one summary naming the count and the senders instead of one interruption per message. Ask the bot to read the backlog and it is all still there, until the usual retention window sweeps the folder.

- Held mail from an unknown sender no longer floods your notifications. The approval question was asked once per message, so fifty messages meant fifty questions to answer one at a time. Past a few from the same sender you now get a single notice covering the rest; each message is still held and still individually approvable, and that notice is sent even if you were already told about throttled mail in the same period.

- The server now has swap, and the bot's own services have a memory ceiling. Without swap the kernel cannot reclaim cold memory at all, which is how one ordinary job took the whole machine down in August: it had nowhere to put anything, so it threw away the cache every program was running from and the box spent 41 minutes reading itself back off disk. Compressed in-RAM swap gives it somewhere to put things, and it adds no disk traffic on a machine whose disk was already the bottleneck. Operators who arranged swap another way can set `istota_zram_enabled: false` and nothing changes.

- The scheduler now records the host's memory state every five minutes, whether or not anything is wrong. A production host was lost in August to memory that had been quietly accumulating for five days with nothing recording any of it, because a slow leak crosses no alarm threshold until the day it is fatal. Each line separates memory some filesystem can be asked about from memory that lives in none — the distinction the August investigation could not make, and the reason it never named a culprit. `host_pressure_enabled = false` turns it off.

- The test suite can now be run on Linux, inside the sandbox it is meant to describe. Everything the bot knows about its own isolation was asserted on a Mac, where the sandbox cannot run at all — the tests checked the command line that was meant to build it and never the result. `scripts/test-linux.sh` runs the whole suite in a container with a real kernel and a real bubblewrap, and a handful of new tests check what is actually true inside the boundary: the databases are absent and cannot be written, another user's files are not there, a network-isolated task reaches nothing but its proxy. It needs Docker and nothing else does — the ordinary `pytest` run is unchanged, and a machine without Docker runs and develops against the full suite as before. Its first run found three real defects, below.

- **The bot can now see the images you send it.** An image arrived as a file path and nothing else, so a screenshot worked only when the text in it happened to be readable and a photograph was answered from the filename and whatever the conversation implied. Every image is now decoded, turned the right way up, resized and given to the model as a picture, with an automatic OCR pass beside it for the exact strings — account numbers, codes — that reading pixels gets wrong.

- Where an image cannot be shown, you are told which one and why instead of getting a confident answer written blind. A model without vision support, a file that will not decode, one past the size limits, OCR cut short by its budget: each is named in the request, and a reply written without an image says so at the bottom. When the bot is running on the Claude CLI it is required to open each image before answering, and the reply says which ones it never opened.

- Sending several images no longer holds up the reply. Each image is read by its own short-lived process, and those now run four at a time instead of one after another: five images take about two seconds of preparation where they took about five. Each of those processes was also loading a calendar client and an HTTP library before reading a single pixel, because of the module the OCR was reached through; it now starts from a small module that loads only what it needs, which cuts each process's start-up from 0.41 to 0.23 seconds.

- **A task run on the bot's own model harness now writes down what it actually did.** Every attempt gets its own file holding the whole conversation: the prompt as sent, each tool call with what it returned, the model's reasoning, where the context was compacted, any mid-run steer, and how the run ended. Until now only the tool *names* survived a task, so a wrong answer could not be reconstructed after the fact; the Claude CLI backend already kept a transcript of its own, and this closes the gap. Off with `[brain.native.session_log] enabled = false` — note that switching it off also stops the clean-up below, so anything already written stays until you delete it.

- Four commands read those transcripts back: `istota session list`, `show`, `tail` and `stats`. `show` and `tail` take a bare task id as well as a path, and `tail` follows a run in progress. A damaged or half-written file is reported as such rather than rendered as though it were the whole run.

- **The bot can read the transcript of one of its own earlier tasks when you ask it why something went wrong.** `istota-skill tasks transcript <id>` returns a digest first — turns, tool calls, sizes, how it ended — with `--tools`, `--turn N` and `--grep` to fetch just the part that matters. It only ever reaches your own tasks, never the run it is in the middle of, and every tool result comes back marked as untrusted content it must not act on.

- Transcripts hold your prompt and everything the tools read, so they sit on local disk beside the databases, readable only by the bot's own user, and no task is given them to read as files — the bot reaches them through the command above instead. On the Docker deployment, where tasks already run without the sandbox, that separation is the only thing keeping them apart. They are deleted after fourteen days, and a size ceiling across all users (2 GB by default) keeps a busy week from filling the disk the databases are on; `istota doctor` reports where they are, how much space they use, and whether the ceiling rather than the fourteen days is what is actually clearing them.

### Changed

- The rules deciding what each messaging surface may do with a room now live in one table instead of ten hardcoded lists of surface names spread over five files. Nothing changes for anyone using the bot; what changes is that those lists agreed with each other by luck rather than by design, and three genuinely different questions were being asked of the same names — the ground the promoted-room fault above grew in. Each question now has one name and one answer per surface, and a test refuses a new surface that leaves one of them unanswered. The stand-in for Nextcloud Talk used in testing also refuses a room address the real Nextcloud would refuse, so a misroute of that kind now fails a test rather than going quiet in production.

- The health check no longer tests a program the install does not use. Both `!check` and the nightly self-check ran the `claude` command line to prove a model answers, whatever backend was actually configured. On the built-in agent loop, which is what the Docker deployment ships, that exercised a program nothing else touches and said nothing about whether tasks would work. It now names the backend in use and reports that there is no command line to exercise. An install running the Claude Code or tmux backend is unaffected.

- A run of failed tasks in the last hour is now reported as a warning rather than raising an alert. It reads the same way in the health report and in the nightly self-check, and the self-check no longer treats it as a reason to notify you: a task that failed an hour ago is worth seeing and is not worth a page. Everything else the self-check reports is unchanged in that respect.

- A health problem that has not changed since it was reported is now reported once a day rather than once an hour. The nightly self-check reads the full health report, and some of what that report can fail on is the documented normal state of a deployment — the Docker stack runs without the sandbox, and says so. Under the old rule, which rate-limits a repeat rather than ending it, that would have notified every user with a self-check once per cooldown indefinitely. It now stays quiet while the set of failing checks is unchanged, up to a daily ceiling so that a problem quietly getting worse still resurfaces. A recovery clears the record, so the same problem happening again next month is reported again. The other five check types are unchanged: a website that is still down an hour later is still worth saying twice.

- The heartbeat sweep no longer runs on the scheduler's main loop, so a slow health check no longer stops the bot picking up work. It used to run there, which was fair when the six check types were cheap and stopped being fair when the self-check grew into a full health report — process launches, a connection per configured service, and optionally a live model call, once per user. It runs on its own thread now, like the database sweeps and the nightly memory passes, and a sweep still running when the next one is due is skipped rather than stacked. It also saves each check's result as it goes instead of all of them at the end, which keeps it from holding the database against everything else while it works, and means a restart mid-sweep no longer re-sends notices it had already sent.

- `!check` now prints the full health report instead of five lines of its own. It carried its own copy of five probes that had drifted from what `istota doctor` reports and from the nightly self-check's copy of the same five; all three read one report now, so a check in a room and a check on the host answer alike. The report comes back inside a code block, since its alignment is what makes it readable and posting it as ordinary text ran the lines together. Someone who is not an admin gets a one-line verdict rather than the list: the full report names plan utilization, other people's user ids and other people's checkouts, none of which the five lines carried.

- **Failing over to a second model backend now happens only where you asked for it.** One of the three backends carried a failover target nothing in your configuration named: leave the setting empty and it still switched to the Claude CLI, so there was no way to say "no failover" for it, and the two warnings about a mistyped or self-referential setting both claimed to be turning failover off while it stayed on under a different name. Every backend is treated alike now, and an empty setting means what it says. A new install writes the old target in for you where that backend is in use, so nothing changes unless you clear it. An **existing** install keeps the configuration file it already has, which for that backend means no failover from now on — the bot says so once at start-up every time it loads, since it is a combination you could not previously have. Add the setting back if you want the old behaviour.

- **A backend going down is now reported whether or not you have a failover configured.** The alert, and the record the nightly memory pass reads to stop hammering a dead backend, were both attached to the reroute, so an install with no failover got neither — the only sign was tasks failing. They now fire on the failure itself, and say there is nothing to reroute to.

- Sending a photograph no longer files a transcription note about it. Any image with no explicit request was treated as "transcribe this and save it", which for a picture of a dog is neither useful nor what the sender meant. Transcription happens when you ask for one; an image sent with any other request is answered as what it is.

- **A message waiting to be sent in web chat now offers Edit and Remove as icons rather than as words.** The line under the bubble already says what the message is waiting for, and two more words after it read as part of the sentence rather than as things to press. Send is unchanged and keeps its word: it appears only on a message the bot is holding for you, where it is the decision being asked for.

- **The web chat offline notice is now a warning in the notice band rather than a row on the composer.** Docked to the composer it read as a caption on the text box rather than a statement about the app. It sits in the band that slides down from under the app bar, which floats over the page instead of pushing it down, so the conversation is exactly as long with no connection as with one — on a phone the intermediate design cost about a fifth of the visible messages for one line of text. It stays up for as long as you are offline rather than disappearing after a few seconds, and it steps aside if something else needs to be reported, coming back when that has been read.

- **A scheduled job whose script has broken now says so, instead of quietly producing the usual result anyway.** The bot was told both to be resourceful and to surface what failed, and the first was winning: when a step died it rebuilt that step's work by hand and delivered the expected artifact, so a job broken for a week arrived looking healthy every morning. Reporting the failure in what you actually read is now a flat rule, and on unattended work standing in for a broken script by hand is out — where you asked for the result in the moment the bot may still fill the gap, but it has to say what broke and what it substituted.

- **Your own additions to a skill's instructions are now edited as files, not through a command.** Those files are yours to write, and one of them is a twelve-step coding workflow with paragraphs and code blocks in it — which the old commands could not write at all, since they only added, removed or reworded a single bullet. Ask the bot to change one, or open it in Nextcloud.

- Two read-only commands remain and have moved: `istota-skill skills overlay <name>` prints one, and `istota-skill skills overlays` lists what you have customized and whether each file actually loads. Run the second after an edit — a file that does not load is otherwise completely silent, and it says which of the four reasons applies.

- Searching your memory finds these files on a schedule now rather than the instant they change. The old update ran from the write command, which never covered editing the file yourself — the way it was always meant to be done.

- **Briefing schedules are now the settings page and the operator's config, and nothing else.** `BRIEFINGS.md` in your config folder is no longer read. It used to win over everything: a time or a room you set on the settings page was overridden by whatever that file said, while the page went on showing you the value you had picked. Content had already moved to the block editor, so all the file still controlled was the schedule and where it went — and it controlled those in a way nobody could see.

- **Upgrade note:** on first start after the update, whatever your file held is copied into the settings, once, and the file's time, room and delivery choice win over what is stored — so no briefing you were actually receiving changes when or where it arrives. From then on the settings page is the only authority and your edits stick. Your own `BRIEFINGS.md` is left where it is; it does nothing now and can be deleted, and new setups get no such file. The `[briefings.components]` toggles some files still carry are not copied over: nothing has read them since content moved to blocks, and importing them would add content you have not been getting.

- Two things do change, both of them a setting of yours that the file had been quietly ignoring. A briefing you switched off in the settings page was still being run from the file, and now stays off. A title you typed there was being replaced by one derived from the briefing's name, and now shows as you wrote it. If a briefing you expected stops arriving, check whether it is switched off.

- **Three machine-kept files no longer sit in your config folder.** The bot tracked its own edits to your memory file in `USER.md.audit.jsonl`, plus two small bookkeeping files beside it. That folder is one you read and edit by hand, and none of the three was for you. All three are now rows in the bot's own database. Nothing you did with that folder changes, and the record itself is unchanged — same entries, same fields, just somewhere else.

- Two consequences worth knowing. The record is harder to lose: that folder is handed to the bot's own tasks to write in, so a task could previously delete its own edit history, or forge the fingerprint the nightly check uses to notice edits made outside the normal path. It can no longer reach either. And the bot's key-value tool now refuses any name starting with an underscore, on reads as well as writes, and leaves those names out of its listing — that is where this state lives.

- **Upgrade note:** the nightly pass imports the three files and then removes them, on its own, the first time it runs after the update. It does this whether or not nightly memory tidying is switched on. A file it cannot fully read is imported as far as it goes and left in place rather than deleted, so nothing is lost silently. Read the imported record with `sqlite3 <db> "select key, value from istota_kv where user_id = '<you>' and namespace = '_memory_audit' order by key"`.

- **The coding skill no longer tells you how to work.** It carried a twelve-step routine — worktree, change tiers, when tests get written, a verification budget, a review before landing, a report template — that was one contributor's habits shipped to every install. That routine is gone, and with it 22 KB of the skill. What is left is machinery: how the repositories, the forge commands and the build container on this deployment work, and what goes wrong if you use them incorrectly.

- **A per-skill file no longer gets flagged for being long.** The warning fired above 8 KB, set when these files were expected to hold a few preference lines. The change above makes a 20 KB coding workflow the normal shape for one, so the warning fired on exactly what the design asks for — on every load of the skill, and as a standing yellow in `istota doctor` for as long as the file existed. It now warns above 24 KB, where the only thing it means is that the 32 KB limit past which the file stops being read at all is close. On the load path it is no longer written to the log at all; `doctor` reports it once, which is where it belonged.

- **Tests and reviews now happen when you ask for them.** A request to fix a bug is a request to fix a bug. If you want a process, write one — in your memory file, in the coding skill's own per-user file, or in a project room's memory — and the bot follows it. Nothing is imposed by default, and the parts that were never negotiable (the forge boundary, the network allowlist, the credential rules, every delete path) are unchanged.

- The skill stated a 600-second ceiling on a single command three times, and nothing in the bot sets that number: it belongs to one of the model backends, while another caps at 120. A deployment that falls back to the second would have had a task plan a run against five times the budget it actually had, get killed partway and produce nothing — the exact failure the advice existed to prevent. The advice no longer names a number and says to detach anything that might not comfortably fit.

- **The bot's coding routine is now yours to set.** It used to be fixed: a separate working copy for every task, a three-level system deciding how much process a change got, a whole test suite at the end of the work, a review before anything landed, and a merge request rather than a merge. None of that was chosen by the people running it. All of it is still what happens by default, but writing a development workflow in your own memory file now overrides it, and writing one in a project room's memory overrides it for that project. Where both say something and they disagree, the room wins for work started there. Nothing new to configure — those two files already existed and are already read at the start of every task.

- **A coding task no longer runs the whole test suite at the end.** It runs the tests covering what it changed, plus the linters and type checker over the whole project, which is what makes a narrow test run safe. This is the one default that changed rather than merely becoming overridable: on a smaller machine the old rule could not finish inside a single command, so the run was killed partway and the task committed nothing — less coverage than a narrow run that completed, after far more waiting. A task now says which command it ran, over which files, what the exit status was and what it did not cover, so a partial pass reads as a partial pass. If your machine can finish the full suite and you want it, ask for it in your memory file and you will get it.

- Reports from a coding task gained a line naming whose rules the task was working under — your memory file, a room's, or the shipped defaults. Two tasks on the same install can now run under different rules, and without that line there is no way to tell a narrow test run that was asked for from one that was careless.

- Deployment mechanics did not become negotiable and are unchanged: the network allowlist, the limits on what the code-hosting commands may do, where builds and tests run, the credential rules, the time ceiling on a single command, and every path that deletes something. An instruction that collides with one of those is reported back rather than followed.

- A `WORKFLOW.md` now appears in the examples folder of your bot directory, listing each decision you can set and where to write it. It arrives on the next start. As with everything in that folder it is rewritten each time to stay current, so write your own workflow in your memory file rather than in the example.

- Everything the dev-container tool does, apart from resetting the box, now goes through that same channel instead of through Docker. It gets real exit codes — a failing command reported as a success was the defect this whole subsystem was built to remove — along with the full output, no timeout of its own, and a straight answer when the container has gone away rather than a fabricated success. Copying files in and out is decided inside the container, by the process that can actually see the filesystem, rather than against a hand-kept list of paths on the outside that had twice been wrong. A copy that fails partway now leaves nothing on your disk and says so, instead of handing back a fragment reported as complete. The scratch area held in RAM is gone; use the container's home directory, which is where commands already start.

- **The dev container and the coding work now switch on together, as one setting.** They were two, and either could be on with the other off. With the container on and the backend off, every dev-container command but `reset` refused on a machine where nothing was wrong; the reverse pointed the coding work at a container no deploy had built. Where builds run is now worked out from whether the container is enabled, so the two cannot disagree.

- **Upgrade note:** a config file still naming the old backend setting is ignored, with a warning at startup and from `istota doctor` rather than in silence. If you set it to keep builds on the host, turn the dev container itself off instead — otherwise your next deploy starts running builds inside it. A deploy rewrites the config for you, but a hand-edited file, a settings file or an inventory variable still naming it needs the change made by hand.

- Packet-level network diagnostics no longer work inside the dev container, on any deployment. `traceroute`, `mtr` and `tcpdump` need a raw-socket capability the container no longer holds: a build needs none of them, and a container holding it can pick its own source address and walk past the address-scoped firewall rules that keep it off your private network. Anything working over an ordinary socket is unaffected, `ping` most likely included.

- The dev container is built on Debian 13 instead of Debian 12, and its two pinned toolchains move with it: Node 20 to 24, Go 1.22 to 1.26. The system packages inside the box go forward with the base — Python 3.13, ImageMagick 7, ffmpeg 7. A deploy rebuilds the image on its own; the container's home directory is a volume and survives, and anything you had installed with `apt` inside the box does not, as it never did across a rebuild. The web bundle the bot serves is still built and run on Node 20, which is a separate pin and not part of this.

- The Linux test tier runs on the host when the host is already Linux, instead of building a container to borrow a kernel it is standing on. The container is not only slower there, it tests less: inside it bubblewrap runs as root and so never creates the user namespace the real deployment relies on, one hardening flag cannot be exercised at all, and the permission-bit tests skip. Nothing changes on macOS, which lands on exactly the path it did before. The mode is picked automatically and can be pinned with `ISTOTA_LINUX_TIER_MODE=auto|native|container`.

- Running that tier on the machine hosting a live deployment now stops and says so rather than picking the host. The tier claims every core and creates real namespaces beside the running bot, so it refuses when it finds an active service or an installed config file, and names the two ways to proceed deliberately.

- The Docker compose stack no longer ships a devbox container, because nothing in that shape could reach one. The skill CLI shells in with `docker exec` from inside the istota container, which has no docker client and no docker socket, and the generated config there has no `[devbox]` section to switch the skill on with — so the service only ever obliged every change to the Ansible devbox to be mirrored into a container nobody could use, which is how it came to be missing a credential socket in the first place. Devbox work goes through the Ansible deployment, which is unchanged.

- **Upgrade note:** the upgrade itself does not remove the container you are running, but the next run of the rebuild script does — it takes the stack down with `--remove-orphans`, and a container whose service has left the file is what that removes. Anything the box accumulated lives in its writable layer rather than in its home volume, so copy out or commit whatever you want to keep before then. The home volume and the network survive the change and can be removed by hand; the Docker deployment guide has the commands.

- `istota_developer_cli_floors` is gone. It asserted a minimum `gh` and `glab` version after install, but sat below what the Debian archive ships — so the stale install it was meant to catch cleared it, and it was a third place to edit on every version bump. gitleaks keeps its minimum under the new name `istota_developer_gitleaks_min_version`: below that one the credential scan does not run at all and every unattended commit on the host is refused. A floor set in your inventory is now ignored.

- A server install now gets the same `gh` and `glab` the container images ship, taken from GitHub's and GitLab's own releases at a pinned version and checked against a known hash before installing. It was taking both from the Debian archive, which on the current release means versions roughly fifty and sixty releases behind what the rest of the project pins, though nothing had actually failed. The binaries land in `/usr/local/bin`, ahead of the packaged ones on the search path, so the older packages are left in place rather than removed.
