A missing one is normally unremarkable — you get a database per feature you actually use — but one that had a snapshot last week and no file today is coverage shrinking, which is what went unnoticed above. It alerts for a week and then stops, rather than repeating for the life of the install.

- The bot no longer reports a test suite as green when it failed. Its own instructions told it to trim a long test run down to the last few lines, which on a shell pipeline reports whether the trimming worked rather than whether the tests passed — so a run that failed a dozen tests exited successfully and was written up as a clean pass. Twice this month the real result was caught only by someone reading the output. The instructions now say to keep the runner's own exit status, and the summary it writes has to name the status it read.

- Picking which of your older messages to load is a model call too, and it was the one call the spend figures never saw — the most frequent thing the bot asks a model on its own, cheap per call but nobody knew how many. It went out through a private path that predated the rest, so it also missed the switch that closes an add-on the underlying tool can be configured to run, and setups with that configured were paying for it on every turn. It now takes the same route as every other call, reports what it spent under its own label, and gives up inside the timeout you configured instead of retrying past it while you wait.

- A coding task can create a working tree again in the repositories the bot clones for itself. Setting one up wrote a state that made the next step — creating the tree the task actually edits in — fail outright with "HEAD not found below refs/heads", leaving the task nowhere to work. The state was written once, at clone time, and stayed on disk, so every repository already set up this way was affected too; those are repaired on the next pass rather than needing to be cloned again. The same pass records which branch a repository treats as its default, so a project on `master` is no longer worked against a `main` that does not exist, and a project whose default branch was renamed is picked up rather than pointing at the old name.
- A reviewer that fails no longer blocks the bot from publishing the branch it reviewed. When every reviewer's call failed, or every reviewer answered in something that would not parse, the result was reported the same way as a bad request — and the rule for a bad request is to stop and open nothing, so one broken reviewer could block every merge request on an install for a reason no branch could do anything about. Those cases now land the work unreviewed and say why, as already happens when the reviewer cannot be reached at all. A review that called the model and came back with nothing also writes a warning to the log, which it previously did at no level; without it, a reviewer broken this way would now be invisible rather than merely obstructive.

- A code review now reports the budget each reviewer was given, and quotes it when the review ran short. A budget that will not fit under the proxy's own ceiling is cut down to one that does, and nothing said so — the warning went to the server log, so a review with a third of its intended time looked exactly like one that had all of it. The cut itself was wrong in two ways worth knowing if you set these by hand: a budget already under the ceiling could be revised upwards, and a zero or negative one reached the model unchanged, which on one of the two brains means no time limit at all.

- The documented way to run a code review named a branch that does not exist in the repositories the bot works in, so copying the example produced a range error and blocked the merge request it was meant to precede. The example now omits the branch and uses the default, which was already right.

- The bot's own model calls no longer come back as machine output instead of an answer. Newer versions of the underlying tool changed the shape of what they return, and the reply was read only in the older shape — so the nightly memory pass, briefing sections, document reading and the code reviewer each received a block of internal bookkeeping where the answer should have been, and none of their spend was counted. Code review was the visible casualty: every diff came back unreadable, which the bot reads as a reason not to open the merge request. Both shapes are now accepted, and a shape belonging to neither is logged rather than passed along silently. The recovered spend is reported under an unknown cost basis, because the newer shape does not say which credential paid for it — so it is counted, but shown separately from the metered and subscription figures rather than added to either.

- The bot can open GitLab merge requests again. Seven of its GitLab recipes filtered command output with `--jq`, a flag `gh` has and the `glab` on a standard server install does not, so each exited with an error instead of a value — including the required check on the destination project, which then compared an empty string, aborted every time, and reported it as GitLab returning the wrong project rather than as a flag that does not exist. The recipes now read those fields in a way that works on every `glab` version the deployment installs, and the reads that feed a later command abort instead of passing an empty value on. Only GitLab was affected; the GitHub side was always correct.

- The docker image now ships the `gh` and `glab` the developer skill needs. Its git half worked — clone, branch, commit, push — but every command that would publish the work failed with "cannot run /usr/local/bin/gh", because neither binary was in the image; anyone who had set a repository directory and a forge token hit it, and a stock install with neither set was unaffected. Upgrading an existing container is enough to fix it, with no config change needed.

- The GitLab reviewer setting takes a username, and every place it was documented asked for a numeric user ID — including the example value, which was a number. The setting is handed to `glab mr create --reviewer`, which wants a username, so an operator who followed the example got merge requests with no reviewer on them and a note about the misconfiguration on each one. The setting itself is unchanged; check yours if it holds a number.

- The server's own logs no longer fill its disk. Neither the system journal nor the audit log shipped with a working limit — the journal's was left to a default of a tenth of the whole disk, and the audit log was set to rotate forever and never delete, so it had kept every file since April. Both are now capped, the audit files stranded above the new limit are removed on the next deploy, and `istota_journald_manage` or `istota_auditd_manage` set to false hands either one back to you.

- Old versions of the Claude CLI are now cleaned up. It updates itself and leaves each previous build behind at roughly 320MB, and nothing ever removed them. The two newest are kept, so there is still one to fall back to after a bad release, and the build currently in use is never removed even when it is the older one.

- Database backups now keep separate windows for the copies on the server and the copies on your Nextcloud. Both were governed by one setting, so shortening the local window to save disk also quietly cut the off-server history — the copies that exist precisely because the server's disk can be lost. The local window is unchanged in spirit but now stated separately, and it stays long enough to outlast a Nextcloud outage, since a snapshot skipped while the mount is down is never copied up afterwards.

- Stopping a task now stops what it started. Cancelling from chat or the web, and a task hitting its time limit, killed only the assistant process — anything it had launched went on running with nothing left to stop it, so a cancelled test run kept every core on the server busy until the next restart. Those three paths now stop the whole tree together. Background work the bot starts on its own, such as the nightly memory pass, is not covered yet and still only stops its top-level process.

- A server installed with Ansible now gets its Talk channels created for it. The `#general`, `#logs` and `#alerts` rooms were only ever set up by the Docker installer, so a bare-metal install started with no logs channel and the execution log quietly off, while the same install under Docker had it working — and the setting that turns it on asked for a room token the operator had no way to know yet. The rooms are created on deploy now and their tokens filled in. A channel you already set is left alone, and so is one you deliberately cleared: turning the execution log off in the web UI stays off across deploys rather than being switched back on. `istota_provision_talk_rooms: false` turns the whole thing off.

- The `!command` suggestion list in web chat no longer stays on screen after you send. Sending empties the field without the browser reporting a change, and the list only ever recomputed from what it saw you type, so it sat over the composer offering the command that had just run until the next keystroke cleared it. It now follows the text itself — a change to the field the list did not see closes it, whatever made the change — which also stops the off-screen list taking the next Enter and putting the sent command back in the empty field.

- Setting up a repository the bot has not worked on before now works. The step that picks which branch to start from carries a fallback for servers that do not advertise a default, and that fallback never ran — so the bot tried to branch from nothing and git refused with an error pointing nowhere near the cause. A second fault in the same step left stale copies of the default branch behind, which is how the bot came to read source frozen at the day it cloned rather than the current version.

- An invoice now records the date it went out, and payment auto-matching uses it. Nothing stored an issue date, so the guard against a bank credit settling an invoice that did not exist yet fell back to the last work billed — weeks early on a month invoiced in arrears, which let any credit from that gap through. Invoice lists also showed the *earliest* work on an invoice under a column labelled its date. Both now read the real date. Invoices raised before this have no date to read and nothing can reconstruct one, so they keep an estimate — the latest work billed, which is also what the listed date now shows for them instead of the earliest. A payment that fits an old invoice to the cent but predates it is no longer settled silently either; it is reported for you to decide.

- Approving a held action from web chat no longer erases the record of what led up to it. Whatever the agent had already done before it stopped to ask — the tools it ran, the steps it took — was the only lasting account of that first pass, and granting permission deleted it, so the finished task showed only what happened after you said yes. Approving the same task from a Talk room never did this. The record now stays and the resumed run adds to it.

- Work is now handed out to whoever has been waiting longest. Users were scanned in no particular order and the scan stopped once the machine was busy, so on a host with more people waiting than free slots the same person could be skipped over and over. Three users with work is enough to reach this on default settings.

- Mail that arrives in a burst is no longer lost. Each check looked at the fifty newest messages in the mailbox and nothing else, so anything pushed below that line between two checks was never read — no error and no log line — and it stayed buried, because the next check looked at the same fifty. A busy mailing list or a run of notifications was enough to do it. Checks now work forward through the mailbox in arrival order and resume where they left off, so a backlog drains instead of burying what sits underneath it. This stops mail going missing from here on; it deliberately does not go back and answer mail that was already missed, since replying to months-old messages would be worse than leaving them.

- A message the mail server refuses to hand over is retried for a few minutes before being set aside, and setting it aside is now recorded rather than silent. A dropped connection mid-check used to mean the message was simply skipped, and it no longer stops the messages behind it from being read.

- A slow or unreachable mail server no longer holds up everything else. Checking for mail ran on the same thread that hands work to the agents, and kept the database locked while it downloaded attachments and uploaded them to your files, so one unresponsive mailbox stopped tasks from starting for every user on the machine. The check now runs on its own, and holds the database only for the moment it records a message.

- Moving to a new mail server, or having a mailbox rebuilt, no longer makes incoming mail invisible. Messages are identified by a number the server assigns, and those numbers start over when a mailbox is recreated, so every new message looked like one already handled and was skipped. The record now tracks which mailbox a number came from.

- A vaccine you have never recorded a dose of now reads "Never recorded" instead of "Series incomplete". Any vaccine given as a series was labelled part-done from the start, so an empty immunizations page opened on ten vaccines that looked half-finished, and the health summary the bot reads listed all ten as needing action. "Series incomplete" now means what it says: you started the series and have doses left.

- A reply to mail the bot sent for you now shows up in your own mailbox listing. When a correspondent answers the bot's plain address rather than your personal one, the reply was still delivered and answered normally, but listing your mail did not show it — so the one query you run when you suspect something went missing was the query that hid it. Searching your mail found it, and still does. The listing now looks for replies to your last twenty-five or so messages as well; searching remains the way to find a reply to something older than that.

- "Last active" in the admin user list now means the last time the person used the bot. It counted scheduled jobs, briefings and module pollers too, so anyone with a cron entry read as active a few minutes ago whatever they had actually done — on a busy host the column reported the scheduler rather than the user. Someone whose only traffic is automated now shows a dash. Their task total is unchanged; that one counts everything on purpose.

- A private email exchange with the bot is now kept out of the room's context and memory, not just out of what the room shows. Keeping it off the transcript stopped it being visible there, but the room's history, its recall and its nightly summary all still counted it — so the quoted thread went on being read back into every later task in that room, which was the cost the change was meant to remove. Mail from an outside contact is unaffected in all three.

- A request you email in that fails now tells you it failed. Replying from your own address is answered by mail alone, and errors are never emailed, so a failed request went nowhere at all: no message in the room, no mail, nothing. You now get a notification wherever your alerts go. A scheduled job that mails a report out stays silent as before.

- An answer that can't be emailed is no longer lost. When mail is the only destination and the send fails, the reply survived only in the task record; it is now sent to you through your alerts instead, with the answer itself included.

- A private email exchange with the bot is no longer copied into the room the conversation started from. Replying from your own address only needs an answer by mail — you are already reading the thread — but every message and every reply was also written into that room as an ordinary turn, quoted chain and all, so a four-message exchange left several copies of itself there. Mail from an outside contact still appears in the room, which is the case the copy exists for. Answering such a reply with a question of your own now comes back by mail rather than waiting for you in the room.

- A reviewer that asks to see a file it was not given now has time to read it. The budget a deploy wrote covered about one call to the model, so the second call — the one that reads the requested file — was started, charged, and then cut off before it could answer; the review still stood on its first answer and said so, but the extra round was paid for and thrown away. A deploy now writes a budget large enough for both rounds, sitting just under the ceiling the review would otherwise be clamped to.

## [0.40.1] - 2026-08-12

### Fixed

- A newly provisioned user could not sign in until the services were restarted by hand, meeting "Access denied: user not configured" on a perfectly good account. Adding a user through the playbook in fact restarted nothing at all: the step that writes the account was marked as never changing anything, so neither the scheduler nor the web interface was ever told to pick it up. Both are now restarted when a user is actually created or changed, and left alone when nothing was.

## [0.40.0] - 2026-08-10

### Added

- Held email is now answerable from the web dashboard without leaving the conversation. A card appears under the turn that wrote the message, showing the recipients, the subject and the full drafted text, plus anything else that task did — a calendar event it created, say, so declining does not quietly leave one behind. Send, edit the wording, or discard. A draft from a scheduled job with no conversation of its own appears above the transcript instead, so nothing is only reachable from a room you never open.

- Email to someone you have not trusted is no longer sent on the bot's own judgement. It is written, held, and shown to you to approve, edit or discard, and approving sends exactly the text you read. Trusted addresses and your own send immediately as before, so briefings and ordinary correspondence are unaffected. `!drafts` lists what is waiting, `!drafts send <id>` releases one and `!drafts discard <id>` bins it; a draft you leave for a day raises a notification rather than expiring, because it is your own unfinished reply. Operators set the floor with `[email] outbound_approval_floor` (`off`, `untrusted` — the default — or `all`, which holds everything not addressed to you); a user may tighten it but not loosen it.

### Changed

- System messages in web chat — alerts, delivered notifications, `!command` output — now sit on the same left margin as every message around them, with the copy / star / reply / delete row under them like any other. They used to start a step to the left and read as a different kind of surface. Their card, an external message's block and a held draft's card also share one text inset now, so a run of them stacks straight.

- A held email pinned above the transcript no longer takes over the top of the chat pane. It spans the full width like the confirmation cards it sits with, rather than stopping short of them, and collapses to four lines — who it is to, the subject, one line of the message, and the buttons — with the whole thing a click away. Two waiting messages used to fill the view and clip their own Send buttons below a scrollbar. The card under the turn that wrote the mail is unchanged and still opens with a screenful. Send, edit and discard all work from the collapsed card, and approving sends exactly the message that was drafted whether or not it was opened first.

- Committing you to something is now its own decision, in the rules the bot carries. Offering a window on your behalf is not consent to a point inside it, and naming a time, place, price or duration you did not name is a confirmation point even when it falls inside something you approved — the test being whether you named the specific thing, not whether the bot feels confident you would have. An instruction to send one message no longer reads as authority over the thread that follows it, and a correspondent who keeps pinging gets an acknowledgment rather than a decision.

- Operators can set the outbound approval floor from Ansible (`istota_email_outbound_approval_floor`), and a user's own policy per user. The gate shipped switched on with no supported way to turn it off, since the playbook overwrites hand edits to the config file. `istota user ensure` gained `--outbound-approval` and `--external-turn-display` to match.

- Files attached to an email are now restricted to the places the bot is allowed to read for you — your own workspace, the conversation's own folder, and the working directory for the task in hand. It could previously be pointed at any file the service itself could read and asked to mail it out. Trusting an address also says what it does in both directions now — `!trust`, the prompt that offers you "yes trust" on a stranger's first message, and the documentation. A trusted address skips the approval question on mail arriving *and* on mail going out, which is worth knowing before you trust someone to stop being interrupted by them.

- Messages in a shared room now say who wrote them. Anyone else's message used to be labelled with your own name, so a room with two people in it read as though you had said everything in it. Existing transcripts change how they read the next time you open them, and mail from an outside contact is named by its sending address rather than appearing as your own words.

### Fixed

- An email exchange now reads as a conversation in the room it was routed to, rather than as a grey system note with nothing above it. The message that came in appears as its own turn, marked as external and collapsed until you open it, and the answer sits under it as an ordinary reply carrying its task, model and tools. Talk and the web dashboard are also given the same text for it: they could previously show different things for the same message, the room showing the bytes mailed to the contact where Talk showed the explanation written to you. Mail from an address you have not corresponded with before is covered too — it used to reach you only as a notification, which is why the external marking never appeared on the case it was built for.

- The outbound approval gate missed the path the bot actually replies on, so mail could reach an address you had declined to trust without ever being held. The check covered the two send commands and not the deferred reply the assistant reaches for when answering the message that created the task — that one is sent later by the server, through a path with no check at all. It is now checked immediately before the message leaves, which is the one point every route passes, so a held reply is held whichever way it was composed. Holding one now raises a notification straight away, since the assistant has usually already told you it replied and a first-contact thread has no conversation for the draft card to appear in. Answering a stranger's first message with a plain `yes` approves reading it and nothing more; the reply is held separately unless you answer `yes trust`.

- An email the server had already accepted could be reported as having failed to send. Some mail servers answer the closing handshake with an error when they are busy, and that arrived after the message was already on its way — so the send was recorded as a failure and anything that retried it delivered the message twice. Sending now also gives up after a minute rather than waiting indefinitely on a server that has stopped answering.

- When the bot stopped mid-task to ask you something while working on an email, the question reached nobody. It could not be mailed to the outside contact, and the room it would otherwise have gone to was excluded too, so the task simply sat there and was cancelled two hours later without you ever seeing it. The question now goes to the room the conversation belongs to. It is still never mailed to the contact.

- A reply from an outside contact could stop arriving once the conversation had run long enough, with nothing to show it had ever come back — no message, no notification, and no trace beyond the mail sitting read in the mailbox. Long threads eventually carry their reply-tracking headers in an encoded form the poller could not read, so it no longer recognised the reply as belonging to a conversation and quietly filed it as unrelated. Those headers are now decoded, and a second one is consulted as a fallback. A reply already missed this way stays missed — it is on record as handled, so it is not retried; forward it back into the thread to pick the conversation up again.

- Mail from a stranger was held for your approval and then asked about in a room you might never open. If you live in the web dashboard you were never asked at all, and the message was dropped a couple of hours later with no notice anywhere. The question now follows wherever you have routed your alerts, web chat shows any unanswered ones in a banner you can approve or discard from, and `!confirm` answers one from any surface.

- Answering "yes" while two requests were waiting approved whichever arrived last, not the one you were looking at. Each question now carries its own number, and a bare "yes" with more than one open lists them and asks which instead of guessing.

- Approving a held email left the room showing the reply with no question above it, and a confirmation left unanswered in web chat blocked that room until it timed out. Both are fixed: approving publishes the question it had been holding back, and sending a new message clears a question you have moved on from, as it already did in Talk.

- The notice that a request timed out never arrived for email, and said nothing about what had expired. It now goes to your alert destination and names the sender and subject, so the message can be found again in the mailbox.

- A Talk room and its web view are one room, but only your conversations crossed over. Alerts, heartbeat notices, reminders and the approval question for held mail landed in Talk alone, so reading in the dashboard you saw nothing — and for the approval question that meant the mail was dropped hours later without you ever being asked. Anything posted to a room now shows in both views of it.

- Replying "yes" in web chat started a new task saying "yes" instead of answering the question, and in the room the question was asked in it discarded that question outright. A bare yes or no now answers wherever you type it, `yes trust` included, and the exchange stays in the transcript after a reload rather than vanishing. Approving still says so on both surfaces — Talk used to say nothing at all until the task's own result turned up.

## [0.39.0] - 2026-08-09

### Added

- You now choose which parts of your Google account the bot gets. Settings → Google Workspace lists Drive, Gmail, Calendar, Sheets, Docs and Chat, each set to no access, read-only, or read and write, and the connect flow asks Google for exactly that. The operator's scope list becomes the maximum any user may pick rather than what everyone is asked for, so a service outside it is shown as not offered instead of failing later at Google.

- The Google Workspace card shows what you actually granted. It reads the scopes back out of your stored connection and reports each service's current access on that service's own row, beside the level you are asking for, marking one you granted only part of. It also says when your grant no longer matches what is being asked for — the state behind "the bot can't see my calendar" — and tells you a reconnect will fix it.

- An operator can configure an advisor model that consults on a task without paying for it on every call. The cheap default model runs as usual, and only at the strategic moments Claude Code's own advisor tool decides matter does the stronger model weigh in. A task carrying its own model pin skips the advisor, since a pinned model that doesn't support the advisor tool at all would otherwise fail the whole task. Anthropic-backed brains only.

- Web chat can reply to a specific message. Hover a turn and pick Reply; the composer shows what you are answering, the sent message renders a quote above it that clicks back to the original, and the model is told which message the reply responds to instead of having to guess from an ordered history. A reply into a Nextcloud Talk-bound room lands there as a real Talk reply, and a reply sent from Talk shows as one in web chat.

- Istota can check on work it handed off. Having queued a background task or a one-off scheduled job, it can now ask how that run went and read the result back, rather than having no sanctioned way to find out. It only ever sees your own tasks, and the surface is limited to admin users.

- Istota's key-value store handles large and long-lived collections properly. A stored value can now be written from a file instead of the command line, which is what a big one used to die on; a collection that only needs recent history can be capped to its newest N entries; and checking many items against a stored set is one call rather than one per item, which is a large speedup for jobs that dedupe against thousands of seen ids.

- Commits are now scanned before they land. A pre-commit hook checks staged content for credentials (via gitleaks) and for private data — a real name, a production hostname, a home directory path, an account number — which has no recognisable shape and so needs a denylist rather than a detector. Enable it per clone with `scripts/setup.sh`. Your own terms go in a gitignored file, because on a public repo a checked-in list of what to redact is itself the leak; documentation placeholders and lines you mark are skipped, and neither scan ever prints the value it found.

### Fixed

- A chat turn that failed or was cancelled could lose its error bubble, leaving the question sitting on screen with nothing under it. The turn's two records are written a moment apart, and when that moment crossed a whole second the transcript treated them as belonging to different pages and dropped the answer. Turns are now placed by when the message was posted rather than when the task row happened to be stamped, so the error stays with its question — and, in the same conditions, no longer renders above it.

- Tasks failed outright on installs that use a custom system prompt. Narrowing what the sandbox can read also took away the prompt file itself, so the model exited immediately with "System prompt file not found". That one file is now made available directly; the configuration file sitting next to it still is not.

- Every task spent two seconds doing nothing before it finished. The two helper services a task runs behind checked whether they had been asked to stop only once a second, and shutting one down waited out that check rather than interrupting it. They are now woken directly, so a reply arrives about two seconds sooner and a busy scheduler gets that time back per task.

### Changed

- Listing a key-value namespace previews long values instead of printing them whole. Asking what keys exist used to dump every value in full, which for a large stored collection crowded out the rest of what Istota was working on. Each value is now shortened with its real size reported alongside, and the whole values are still one flag or one `get` away. The operator command still prints values in full; it gains the same two flags as options.

- The `smart` model tier and the bare `opus` shortcut now resolve to Opus 5 instead of Opus 4.8. A deployment that pins its own model under `[models.aliases]` is unaffected.

- Enter sends a web chat message, and Shift+Enter writes a newline. Almost every message is one line, so the plain key now belongs to the common case; Cmd/Ctrl+Enter still sends, so nothing you had in your hands stops working. On a phone or tablet the return key keeps inserting a newline — there is no cheap Shift there and the send button is already under your thumb. It never sends the key that commits an input-method candidate, and it writes a newline rather than going dead in the cases where it cannot send: a turn already running, a voice message recording, an attachment still uploading. The send button now refuses an in-flight upload too, which used to post the message and leave the file behind.

- The elevation profile has moved in behind "Show details", under the day's stops and trips. It was taking vertical space under the map on every visit for a reading that is supplementary. The day's elevation spread now sits in the stats bar beside the ping and stop counts, so you can see there is a profile to look at without opening the panel.

- The `sandbox_ro_paths` setting now does something. It has always been documented as the way to expose a co-located service's files to tasks, but nothing ever read it from your config, so every install silently ran the built-in default — which happened to point at the directory tree holding the databases. If you have a value set for it, that value takes effect on this upgrade, so check it is as narrow as you meant it to be. The built-in default is now empty, and a malformed value is refused rather than guessed at.

- `sandbox_admin_db_write` is gone, and the skill proxy is now required wherever the sandbox is on. The first let admin tasks write the database from inside the sandbox, which no longer has a database to write. The second is how a skill command reaches one at all, so turning it off while the sandbox is on leaves those commands with nothing to read — that combination now warns at startup. A leftover `sandbox_admin_db_write` in your config is ignored with a warning rather than failing the load.

- The chat sidebar is ordered by latest activity, with the room you last heard from at the top. It used to be fixed in creation order, so a busy room sat wherever it happened to have been made and a long list had to be read to find it. A room moves the moment a message lands in it, whoever sent it and whichever surface it came from; a room nobody has spoken in yet sorts by when it was created, so a new one still appears near the top.

### Added

- Your timezone can now follow you when you travel. Turn on "Update timezone when I travel" in Settings and, once you have settled in a new timezone for about an hour, the timezone field is set to it and you get a message saying so — no more briefings and calendar times running on your home clock for the first days of a trip. It needs the location module, it stays off unless you turn it on, and it never changes anything without telling you. Being in the air over somewhere does not count: it waits until you have stopped moving. If you set the timezone back by hand it stays set.

### Security

- Istota's databases are no longer reachable from inside the sandbox a task runs in. Every task could previously read the framework database and, on a standard install, every user's health, money, location and feeds data too — not through the admin-only mount that looked like the exposure, but through a read-only path added years ago for a co-located service that no longer exists and which happened to contain the data directory. Stored credentials stayed encrypted throughout, so what was exposed was other people's records rather than a way in. The directories are now blanked out unconditionally, as the last thing the sandbox does, so no future mount can quietly re-open them. Reads and writes go through the skill commands as before, which run outside the sandbox and return only your own rows.

- The setting that stops a task from escaping its sandbox into a nested one has been switched on for the first time. It has been in the code since the sandbox work, but it was being asked for in a way bubblewrap rejects, so the check that decides whether the system supports it always came back "no" and the protection was never actually applied. Nothing else was relying on it — what it guards is the last resort behind the databases being absent — but it is now in force, and the check no longer reports a missing feature when it simply could not run.

- The skill command that indexes a file so it can be found by search was reading whatever path it was handed, using the bot's own filesystem access rather than the task's. Since anything indexed can be read back out through search, that was a way to reach files a task is not meant to see, the configuration file among them. It is now limited to your own workspace, the channel a task is running in, and the task's own scratch directory.

- The dev-container copy commands could reach any user's files, not only the caller's. They run with the bot's own filesystem access and accepted any path under the shared Nextcloud mount root, which holds everyone's workspace — so a task could copy another person's documents into its container and read them there. They are now held to the same limits as the search-index command: your own workspace, the channel the task is running in, and the task's own scratch directory. A refused copy also no longer leaves an empty directory behind where it was headed.

- The helper that decides which older messages of a conversation are still worth including now runs with only the credential it needs to reach the model. It was being started with the whole of Istota's own environment — the key your stored secrets are encrypted with, your Nextcloud password, every connected-service token — on a prompt assembled from conversation history. No known path read them back out, but that limit rested on the command-line tool's own defaults rather than on anything Istota enforced.

- Istota now notices when your mail path stops authenticating who sent an email. Leaving the prompt that holds mail from your own address turned off assumes your provider rejects forged sender lines before they ever arrive — usually true, but it lapses quietly when a DNS record is edited or a mailbox moves, and nothing would have told you. Such mail is now checked against the verdict your provider stamped on it and warns when that verdict is a failure; it watches for your mail setup changing under you rather than for an attacker, who can suppress it, and it never holds or blocks a message.

- The setting that holds mail arriving from your own address for approval now works. The sender line on an email is unauthenticated, so anyone who knows your address can put it there, and this setting was the one thing meant to catch that — but it could never fire, because the check it made was the very thing that had brought it there. It now holds such mail until you approve it from Talk, whichever way the message was addressed. It stays off by default, since it also holds every message you send yourself and no deployment has had this behaviour until now; if you had already turned it on believing it worked, it takes effect on this upgrade.

### Fixed

- Non-admin users can search their memory, read their key-value store and check on their own tasks again. Those commands scope every query to whoever asked, but they were being denied the database location on the grounds that withholding it was a boundary — it wasn't, since the location is fixed and derivable, and the only thing it actually stopped was ordinary users getting their own data. The location now goes to the commands themselves rather than to the model, for everyone.

- A task no longer gets a misleading answer when it reaches for a database the sandbox does not have. The directories those files live in are blanked out, but they were writable, so opening a database path there created an empty file and reported "no such table" — which reads as a missing schema and sends the task hunting for something that was never in reach, leaving the stray file behind for the rest of the run. They are read-only now, so the attempt fails at the open and says so.

- A task that needs something its sandbox can't do no longer burns its whole time limit waiting for an answer that could never arrive. Reaching for the database directly fails in a way that reads as a broken command rather than a refusal, so a wait loop treated it as "not ready yet" and ran to the end, after which the reply was written from whatever was left. Istota is now told plainly that the database is out of reach, that a skill command is how to get something done outside the sandbox, and how to wait for something without hanging on it.

- Google Workspace commands now use the account you connected. Connecting from the web settings stored and refreshed the credentials correctly, but nothing ever handed them to the tool that runs Drive, Calendar, Gmail and Sheets, so every command ran signed out — and the route out to Google was closed off alongside. Any skill that sets up its own credentials was exposed to this; Google Workspace was the only one that did.

- Reconnecting a Google account works. The connect flow never asked Google for lasting access, so a second connect — after changing what Istota may see, or after revoking access from your Google account — came back without the credential needed to stay signed in and was refused, leaving nothing but an error in the address bar. It now asks for it outright, which means Google's consent screen appears on every connect rather than only the first.

- Email retention works on a busy mailbox again. The cleanup only ever looked at the hundred newest messages and deleted whichever of those had aged out — which on anything above a dozen messages a day is none of them, so the mailbox grew without bound while every run reported success. It now asks the mail server for the expired messages directly and deletes those. **On upgrade, the first run clears whatever backlog the broken sweep left behind**, so check that `email_retention_days` is the window you actually want before restarting; as before, it deletes everything in the polled folder past the cutoff, not only mail Istota handled. The number of messages about to go is logged first, and a large backlog is cleared a couple of thousand at a time over successive runs rather than in one pass.

- Deleting mail no longer reaches past the messages Istota picked. Both the retention sweep and the delete command marked their messages and then asked the server to clear the whole folder, which also permanently removes anything another mail client has marked for deletion and not yet cleared. They now remove exactly the messages they chose, and a server too old to support that gets the old behaviour plus one warning saying so.

- The record Istota keeps of every email it has already seen is now pruned. It gained a row per polled message and nothing ever removed one, so it grew forever, and most of those rows had long outlived the task they pointed at. Rows are kept for 90 days by default, always for at least as long as the mail itself, so a message still sitting in the mailbox can't lose its record and arrive a second time as new.

- A task that gets picked up again after a crash or a restart no longer repeats work its first run had queued up. Where a run leaves database writes for Istota to apply once it succeeds, an interrupted attempt used to leave those behind for the next one to apply alongside its own — a subtask created twice, an email recorded twice. Only three of the four ways a task comes back were clearing them; the clean-up now happens as the retry starts, whichever way it got there.

- A reply to mail a scheduled job sent now threads back to the conversation that sent it. Mail sent from a scheduled shell command went out fine and the job reported success, but nothing recorded it, so when the recipient replied there was no thread to attach the answer to and it arrived as a new conversation. A note such a job writes is also now coordinated against the nightly memory pass, so the two can no longer overwrite each other, and a job can hand follow-up work to the assistant the same way the other kinds of job can.

  If you write your own scheduled shell commands, two things change for them. What a command saves — a stored value, a health entry — is now kept only if the command finishes successfully, where before it was kept regardless; put the save last if a later step can fail. And a value saved earlier in a command is not readable back later in that same command, since it is filed once the command is done.

- Mail from someone else is no longer read back to the assistant as something you said. Every email turn was labelled with your name — in the conversation history, in what the nightly pass distils into long-term memory, and in what search recalls later — whether it came from you or from an outside contact replying to a message the bot sent on your behalf. So the assistant re-read a stranger's words as your own instructions, contradicting the warning wrapped around the same text, and could write them into memory as things you said. A turn is now attributed to you only when the sender is one of your own addresses, and to the sender otherwise. Only a plain address is ever shown, so nothing a sender chooses to put in the name field can pose as you.

- The elevation profile no longer bottoms out for every hour you spend at home. The phone tracker declares a fixed coordinate while you are on your home network rather than taking a real fix, and the placeholder altitude it sent alongside was stored as though someone had measured it — so a day spent partly at home drew as a square wave with the real terrain squeezed into the top of the box, and a day where nothing happened drew a strip that should not have been there at all. Those points now carry no altitude, so the profile skips them the way the map skips a stretch your phone never sampled, and history already recorded is cleaned up on upgrade.

- Altitude is finally visible. It has been recorded with nearly every location ping all along, but no reader passed it on, so a flight or a drive over a pass read as a flat 2-D line. Your location history now returns it, and the track view draws an elevation profile under the map whenever the day has a real climb in it — a day spent at sea level still gets none, since the spread there is GPS noise rather than terrain. The line breaks into dashes wherever the map does, so a stretch your phone never sampled cannot read as a climb. What the number is measured against depends on the device that reported it, so treat it as good for "that was a climb" and never as an altimeter reading.

- An email continuing a room you already have open in web chat now shows both halves of the exchange. Only the bot's reply was recorded, so the room displayed an answer with no question above it. A reply routed to a different room still arrives there as an out-of-band note rather than a chat bubble, and a fresh email thread stays out of the sidebar entirely — mail the bot merely receives never creates a room. A message still waiting on the untrusted-sender prompt is withheld until you approve it, and an email turn that fails or is cancelled now surfaces the failure instead of sitting there unanswered.

- A message in web chat no longer shows two "working on it" indicators at once. The assistant's spinner was drawn the moment you pressed send, so any send that took longer than a moment sat under a second "Sending…" mark on your own message — and the assistant one was claiming the model was busy on a message that had not yet reached the server. Commands like `!search` run inside the request, so they hit it every time. The reply's spinner now waits until the message has actually landed.

- The date filters on Medical history look like date fields and fit one line. On a phone they were two blank rounded boxes stacked below the type dropdown, with nothing to say they were dates — iOS draws an empty date input as nothing at all. They now sit beside the dropdown at the same height, reading "Start" and "End" until you pick a date. The two fields also hold one width as you pick dates instead of resizing to whatever each value happens to need, and a date too wide for its box is trimmed rather than wrapping the field onto a second line. The Location history filter bar levels up the same way.

## [0.38.0] - 2026-08-01

### Added

- New portfolio positions classify themselves. Importing a snapshot looks each new ticker up in public market data (falling back to reading the export's own descriptions) and files it under the right asset class, sub-class and geography, so the allocation charts work without hand-classifying every symbol. Auto-filled rows are badged in Money settings and editing one makes your version permanent — an automatic guess never overwrites you. An Auto-classify button and a `portfolio autoclass` command fill in symbols imported before this existed.
- The estimated-tax page can be set to your own state, and says where its rates came from. Pick a state under Money → settings → Taxes, including "no state income tax" — which removes the state column entirely rather than showing zeros. Ten flat-rate states and California ship with rates; anywhere else is selectable and you enter the brackets, which the settings page tells you before you pick rather than after. Every set of figures names the authority it was transcribed from and the date it was last checked, and warns you when it is standing in for a year it isn't from.
- Federal and state tax rates can be corrected by hand. Brackets, standard deductions and payroll rates are editable per year and filing status under Money → settings → Taxes, with a revert button per field to go back to the shipped figures. Overrides used to apply regardless of filing status, so one entered while filing jointly quietly carried over after switching to single; they are now scoped correctly, and existing ones are migrated.

- Logs are readable from the web UI. Admin gained a sidebar — Status, Configuration, Logs — and the Logs view shows the application log and per-task lifecycle output without an SSH session: pick a source, filter by level, search the text, page back through history, or follow new entries live. Clicking a task number narrows to that task, and each entry says which user it belongs to. Configuration is a read-only view of the settings the running processes actually loaded; credentials appear only as set or not set and are never sent to the browser.
- Set up location tracking on your phone by scanning a code instead of typing a secret. Settings → Location generates an ingest token and shows it as a QR code; the Istota iOS app scans it and configures itself. The code is shown once, since the token is never readable again afterwards — leaving the page means generating a new one. Generating again immediately cuts off every device using the old token, which is also how you revoke a lost phone, so it asks first. A URL you can paste is shown alongside for the third-party Overland app, which cannot scan.
- The Istota iOS app has a "This device" card under Settings → Location for the background tracker on that phone: on or off, whether it is logging a continuous trace or only arrivals and departures, how many points are waiting to send, when it last sent, and any error. Tracking settings live on the phone rather than in your account, because two phones on one account would otherwise overwrite each other's. On a laptop the section says so in a line rather than disappearing.
- A push notification can be formatted. Ask for a push and the assistant can send bold text, lists, headings, quotes and code blocks instead of one undifferentiated paragraph, which suits a list of failures or a short report. Formatting shows in the ntfy web app; a phone popup still shows the plain source, so the assistant is told to use it only where the shape earns its keep. Nothing changes for a notification that doesn't ask for it.
- The paperwork behind a medical record is kept and attached to it. A scan or PDF imported to create appointments and conditions used to be discarded the moment its text had been read, so the source document was gone; it is now filed against every record it produced and shown on the encounter, condition and immunization pages. You can attach a file to any of those by hand as well, and the same file attached in two places is stored once. Deleting a document stays a manual action in the browser — the assistant can attach and detach, but not delete.
- The dashboard opens with a greeting instead of a wall of identical tiles. It introduces the bot by name, greets you for the time of day where you are rather than where the browser thinks it is, and carries a rotating usage tip underneath. Tips appear only when they are true for your account — the email tip names your own address and is absent when email is off — and the cards below now carry icons and sit at even heights.
- The location date range is a dropdown, with a year-to-date option added. The four range buttons became one control, which leaves room on a phone, and picking your own start and end reads as "Custom range" rather than leaving every preset looking unselected.
- A condition can be linked to every visit it was seen at, rather than only one. Something found by your GP, referred to a specialist and reviewed at a follow-up now carries all three appointments, and shows up under each of them. Link and unlink from either side — an encounter's page lists its conditions, and a condition's entry lists its appointments. Links recorded before this are carried over automatically.
- An attachment you sent in web chat is a link to the file itself. The chip naming it opens the file from inside your own logged-in session — nothing is published and no share is created, the same handover the assistant already uses for a file it produced. A file the browser can't serve you, such as one attached in Talk or another member's upload in a shared room, stays the plain label it was rather than becoming a link that fails.
- Briefing emails arrive as formatted mail with clickable links. Where a news story cites a source that supplied an article URL, that source name is now a link you can tap. The mail carries a plain-text copy alongside the formatted one, so nothing is lost in a client that prefers plain; untick "Send as HTML" under Briefings → settings → Email delivery for plain text only. There is no styling — your mail client's own typography renders it.
- Newsletter stories in a briefing can be cited to the article they came from. Newsletter links used to be thrown away before the briefing was written, so only feed items could be cited; now a newsletter's own links reach the briefing, with its unsubscribe, view-in-browser and social links filtered out and tracking redirects resolved to the real article where that can be done safely.
- Briefings have a title you can set. It appears as the email subject and as the entry in the briefings archive, with the date appended, and both surfaces now show the same thing. Leave it blank and it's derived from the briefing's name. Set it in Settings → Briefings, where the schedule table also gained an edit button — previously a briefing could only be added or deleted.
- A chat message can be copied, starred or deleted. Hovering a message — or tapping it on a phone — reveals the three actions in a row underneath it. Starring is also still at the message's top-right, where a starred message keeps its star visible without hovering. Copy puts the whole reply on the clipboard as the original Markdown, so pasting it elsewhere gives back what was written rather than a wall of formatting, and code keeps its fences; the tool traces a reply ran through are never included. Delete asks first, and removes the message everywhere at once — the room, the All and Starred views, and what the assistant remembers of the conversation. In a room synced with Nextcloud Talk it removes the Talk copy too, and the confirmation says so. Your own messages, replies and command output all work the same way.
- Brief status messages in the web UI appear in one consistent place: a band that slides down from under the section header and retracts on its own. Errors stay until you dismiss them; a message that repeats shows a count rather than piling up. Because it hangs off the header rather than a corner of the screen, it never lands on the chat composer, and it is in the same spot in every section and in the iOS app.

- The Money section tracks your investment portfolio. Upload a Fidelity Portfolio Positions export on the new Portfolio tab and it shows total value, allocation by asset class, account type and geography, holdings with unrealized gains, and value over time across every import — plus what opened, closed and changed between any two uploads. Importing lives on its own Import page — pick the source (Fidelity CSV, or fina's history file as a one-time migration that brings every snapshot in at once) and drop the file. Accounts and symbol classifications are editable under Money settings (accounts can be grouped freely — by owner, purpose, anything — with the group field suggesting labels already in use; account edits save with the page's Save button, classifications are picked from dropdowns, and an excluded account drops out of every chart, reversibly), the assistant can run the same imports and queries, group accounts, and list or change symbol classifications, and re-uploading a file you already imported is always safe.

### Changed

- Buttons, spacing and status labels look the same across the whole web UI. The Health section had its own set of buttons that disagreed with the rest of the app — its main action was outlined where everywhere else it is filled, and its delete buttons were grey until hovered on some pages and red on others. Health now uses the same buttons, notices, tables and status pills as every other section, and spacing across the app has been put on a consistent scale, so small gaps shift by a pixel or two in places.

- Row actions look and work the same everywhere in the web UI. Briefings, content blocks, sources, shared blocks, feed subscriptions and categories, immunizations and diagnoses all put their actions behind the same ⋮ menu the money pages already used, replacing a mix of pencil and cross icons on some pages and text buttons on others. Reorder arrows stay where they are, since burying them would cost a click per nudge.
- Briefings, blocks, sources and shared blocks can be duplicated — an action the old icon rows had no room for. A duplicated briefing copies its blocks and their sources, and arrives switched off under a free name so it can't run before you have looked it over.
- A settings page has one Save button, in the bar at the top. Settings carried three identical-looking buttons, two of which saved the same thing, and a page's edits genuinely span more than one card — so one button now drives them all and asks only the cards holding changes, with an "Unsaved changes" note beside it. Per-record forms, such as an invoicing client or a briefing source, keep their own buttons, since those save one record rather than the page.
- Money settings splits into Connections, Invoicing and Portfolio behind a sidebar. Six cards over three unrelated concerns had grown into one long page where reaching the third meant scrolling past the two you did not want.
- An error in the web UI shows a real page. A failure used to render as a bare "500" that sat in a different place depending on which section you were in; it now names what went wrong, offers a reload and a way back to the dashboard, and shows the underlying message where that says more than the heading does.
- The Istota iOS app stays in portrait when you turn the phone, and a home-screen install on Android follows the same preference. The layout is a single column with a docked composer, and landscape left too little height for it once the keyboard was up. iPad still rotates, as does an ordinary browser tab, which no site is allowed to lock.

### Fixed

- Estimated taxes for 2026 were computed from 2025 law. The 2026 rate tables were copies of the 2025 ones, so the standard deduction was $30,000 against the real $32,200 (married filing jointly), the bracket thresholds were a year out, and the Social Security wage base was $176,100 against $184,500 — with nothing on the page able to tell you. Federal figures for 2025 and 2026 are now transcribed from the IRS revenue procedures that set them, and the page says which document and when it was checked. If you have been using the 2026 estimate, re-check it.
- California's tax figures were several years out of date, in the same way. The single standard deduction was $5,363 against the Franchise Tax Board's $5,706 for 2025, and the bracket thresholds were similarly stale. Corrected against the FTB's published schedules. California has not yet published its 2026 figures — it does so in the autumn — so the page now says plainly that it is showing 2025 figures rather than presenting them as 2026.
- The estimate could ask a resident of a no-income-tax state for a state payment. Under the safe-harbor method the state amount came from last year's state tax without checking whether the state levies any — so someone who moved from California to Texas and left the old figure in place saw a state payment folded into their total, with no state row on the page to explain it.
- The self-employment tax calculation took the Social Security wage-base cap from the rate data, and would have silently dropped the entire Social Security portion — roughly $23,000 on $200,000 of self-employment income — if a future year's data were added without it. It now falls back to the most recent published base, and the shape that caused it is rejected by a test.
- The qualified business income deduction phased out too early. Section 199A measures against taxable income; the calculation used AGI, so the phase-out began a full standard deduction of income sooner than it should have.
- Estimated state payments used California's payment schedule whichever state you were in. California splits its quarterly instalments 30/40/0/30 rather than into equal quarters, and that split was applied to every state's amounts. Each state now uses its own schedule.

- Tapping a text field on a phone no longer zooms the page in. Opening room settings, or any dialog or form in the app, blew the field up to the full width and pushed the rest of the layout off-screen, and iOS did not reliably zoom back out afterwards. Fields and dropdowns are now large enough on touch that it no longer triggers, so the app stays put and pinch-zoom still works when you want it.

- The section dropdown in the app bar on a phone is back to its intended size and no longer sits almost flush against the page title. Making form fields large enough to stop iOS zooming had also enlarged this dropdown, which is navigation rather than a field, leaving it the heaviest thing in a compact bar. It is now the same dropdown the rest of the app uses, sized as designed and spaced off the title. Text fields keep the larger touch size, which is doing real work.

- The Monarch connection form under Settings → Money fits a narrow screen. Each field's label sat beside its input in a fixed column that left no room for the input itself on a phone; labels now sit above their fields. The two ways of connecting are also named for what they are — logging in with an email and password, or pasting cookies from your browser — instead of "Option A" and "Option B".

- The admin Logs toolbar reads as one bar on a phone. The Refresh and Follow buttons now take a row of their own beneath the search field, instead of landing wherever the line happened to wrap and sitting visibly shorter than the fields beside them. The "Logger prefix" box is gone: it needed you to already know a logger's name to be any use, and searching already matches the logger as well as the message, so typing a subsystem name in the search box narrows to it.

- Briefings no longer carry dead links from your notes. A reminder, todo or note whose text linked to another note in your vault arrived with that link intact, so the attribution under a quote was a clickable link to nothing in email and web chat. Those links are now flattened to plain text before the briefing is written, since there was nothing at the other end to reach. Real web links, including the article links under news stories, are untouched.

- Pasting something large into the web chat composer no longer stops drafts being saved everywhere. One oversized unsent message filled the space all drafts share, after which nothing was kept in any room and nothing said so. A very long draft is now held up to a generous limit and loses only its tail, and the store as a whole is bounded, so an unsent paste can no longer take every other room's draft down with it.

- A location track crossing a saved place at speed is drawn as one continuous line. Flying or driving straight through a geofence — an airport, a station — clipped it for a few points, and the map broke the line with a dashed gap on the way in and again on the way out, as if tracking had dropped; the trip list split the journey into fragments to match. Those points are kept in the line when the track was plainly moving through and the points either side are close enough together to show it, so a real break — the phone asleep through a departure, or a long silence in a tunnel — still reads as one.

- A web chat message that failed to send no longer disappears. The failure notice and its Retry were destroyed by anything that rebuilt the transcript — switching rooms, or the reconnect that follows the very outage that caused the failure — leaving no trace of what you wrote. The message now stays put through all of it, and the text is also held as a draft until the server confirms it, so a page reload puts it back in the composer rather than losing it.
- Retrying a message the server had in fact accepted no longer posts it twice. A send that timed out, or whose connection dropped after the server was already handling it, was indistinguishable from one that never arrived; retrying it produced a second copy and a second reply. Each message now carries an identity the server recognises, so a retry resolves to the message already sent.
- A file attached in web chat can no longer end up in the wrong room. Picking a large file and switching rooms before the upload finished put the file's chip in the new room, where sending would have posted it there.
- Sending in one room while another room's message was still in flight could show that message twice, both copies bound to the same reply, and could discard the second room's unsent text while it was still being sent.
- A command that timed out is no longer offered a Retry. A `!` command runs while the request is open, so re-sending it after a timeout could run it a second time.

- A web chat message that fails to send now says so on the message itself, with a Retry. Previously it appeared as an ordinary sent message and the only sign of trouble arrived later, on the reply — so "your message never left" read as "the assistant failed to answer". With the server unreachable it was worse: nothing was reported at all, and the composer stayed stuck showing Stop until the page was reloaded. A message that is slow to send is marked while it waits, a message that fails keeps its text and offers to send it again, and Retry is withheld where it cannot work, such as an expired session or a message the server refused. If the server did accept a message we had given up on, its reply now attaches to that message instead of appearing as a second copy of it.

- Seven headings in the Health section rendered at ordinary body-text size. A missing style meant the heading size was inherited from the surrounding text rather than falling back to a default, so "Bloodwork", "Pending review" and five others read as paragraphs. Health error and warning boxes were also invisible in light mode — the colours were fixed to the dark theme, so a red warning arrived as a dark smudge on white.
- "This module is disabled" notices were green, which reads as a confirmation of something rather than a note that a feature is off. They are blue now, and the Garmin card's "Connected" and "Sync complete" messages are the ones that are green.
- Keyboard focus is visible everywhere. Around two hundred buttons, links and inputs had no focus outline at all, and the few that did drew it in a colour close enough to the resting border to be unseeable — so tabbing through a page gave no indication of where you were. There is now one ring, in the interactive blue, on everything focusable; it appears for keyboard navigation only, never on a mouse click.
- The Health section's "Extracting…" spinner sat above its label on two of the three import screens instead of beside it, and its tables scrolled without momentum on iOS where one of them did. Logging a measurement now opens a proper dialog: Escape closes it, focus is trapped inside it, and focus returns to where you were when it shuts.
- Dropdowns line up with the buttons beside them. A dropdown was about two-and-a-half pixels shorter than an adjacent button, and by a differing amount at each text-size setting, so a row like "link an existing condition" never sat straight.

- Location points are no longer stored twice when a phone re-sends them. An app that keeps points queued until the server acknowledges them will re-send a batch whose reply was lost; those arrivals are now recognised and dropped. Beyond a duplicate row, this had been inflating the length of the visit you were in the middle of.
- Attachments up to 100 MB are accepted on a standard deployment, and an iPhone photo is no longer refused for its format. The old ceiling was low enough to reject an ordinary phone video, and a HEIC dragged from a Mac was turned away on its file extension. The app now hands a picked file to the server directly rather than loading it into the page first, so a large file no longer depends on the browser holding all of it in memory.
- A provider capacity error is no longer handed back as the assistant's reply. "529 Overloaded" and its relatives were not recognised as provider problems at all, so they were never retried, never routed to the backup model, and arrived as the answer word for word. Rate limits, gateway failures and dropped connections are now retried briefly, honouring the provider's own "come back in N seconds", and then run on the backup model where one is configured; if that is unavailable too you get a plain "both my primary and backup brains are unavailable" instead of raw provider text. A request's own fault — a bad model name, an expired key, an oversized prompt — still fails immediately rather than being retried for twenty minutes first.
- A reply is no longer a status note left over from the middle of a turn. Text the assistant writes between tool calls streams live as a progress indicator and was never meant to be kept, but a turn could leave one of those notes standing as the saved answer, ending on a dangling "Let me pin that down" while the actual closing message was dropped. The reply is now the message the assistant finished on. A turn that stops without one says so and shows the last text it produced, instead of passing that off as the answer.
- Logging out asks first. The logout and menu icons sit side by side and are small on a phone, so a mistap ended the session and sent you back through the login screen; a confirmation now stands in the way, and dismissing it — by tapping outside, pressing Escape, or choosing Cancel — leaves you signed in. The top bar's three icons also got larger, better-separated tap targets on small screens.
- Push notifications whose title carries an emoji, an arrow or a dash now arrive. Any such title made the whole notification fail silently, which included every briefing pushed to a phone — a briefing's title has a dash before the date. Titles are sent in an encoding the ntfy server unpacks back to the original characters, so nothing has to be flattened to plain ASCII.
- Connecting Monarch Money with your email and password works again. Monarch has moved browser sign-in to a different address and now turns away anything reaching the old one, so a correct email and password came back as "service unavailable" — it read as an outage rather than something on our side. We now sign in the way Monarch's own web app does, at the address it uses and identifying ourselves the same way. Monarch's refusal blames the app version whatever the real reason, so we also look up the version its web app reports rather than shipping a fixed one, which stops that particular cause from recurring; the value changes within hours, so a fixed one was never going to hold. When Monarch doesn't recognise the connection it emails a six-digit code, which the settings page now asks for — it says your email and password were accepted, so waiting on a code no longer looks like a rejected password, and a code that has expired says that rather than failing silently.
- Importing paperwork that names the same condition at more than one appointment keeps every one of them. The condition was correctly recognised as one condition rather than duplicated, but only the first appointment's link survived — so a later visit disappeared from the condition's history, and the condition disappeared from that visit's page.
- Dropdown lists in the web UI open aligned to the button that summons them and stay inside the page margins on a phone. A long entry wraps within the list instead of widening it off the side of the screen.
- A briefing block reading a todo file can be told to cover only one part of it. The file's headings used to be stripped before the briefing was written, so every item arrived as one undifferentiated list and an instruction like "only show items under NOW" was impossible to follow rather than merely ignored. Items now reach the briefing grouped under the heading they sit beneath, whether the file marks its sections with `### NOW`, an underline, `**NOW**` or `NOW:`.
- A todo file that opens with the usual notes-app header block no longer starts the list with its own tags. Each tag was being read as a todo, so a file tagged "personal" and "errands" led with two items by those names.
- Web chat and Feeds now tell you when something they tried didn't work. Failing to star a message or an article, delete or open a room, mark things read, or follow a link to an older reply all reported nothing at all — the star sprang back, the room stayed put, and there was no way to tell a failure from a misfire. Each of those now says so.
- A very long todo file is trimmed to whole items rather than being allowed to crowd out the rest of the briefing. The size limit that already applied to other sources now applies here too, dropping items from the end and never cutting one in half — a half-item would read as a todo saying something your file doesn't. The block says how many were left out.
- The Admin and Settings pages keep the navigation on screen while you scroll. They were the only two that carried the top bar away with the page, which on a phone or in the app left no way to another section without scrolling back up. Both now name the page in a fixed bar beneath the navigation, as every other section already did.
- A briefing is titled the same way every time. The archive used to show whatever the model decided to call that day's edition, sometimes with an emoji and sometimes not, while the email arrived under a subject worked out from the clock rather than the briefing — so an evening briefing that ran in the morning was mailed as "Morning Briefing", and neither surface named a run the way the other did. Push notifications carried the task number instead of a title.
- The web chat send button is red while a reply is running, instead of staying blue with a stop icon on it. Hovering the button — or, on iPhone, simply having tapped it, since the tap leaves a hover behind that nothing clears — repainted it in the idle blue while the stop icon stayed put, so a running turn looked idle and the button read as two states at once. The colour and the icon are now one thing, and the swap happens instantly rather than fading between them.
- A half-written chat message is still there when you come back to it. Typing in a room and then going to look at something in another section lost what you had typed; it is now kept, per room and on your own machine, until you send it. It also stops a message started in one room from following you into the next, which is what it used to do.
- Scrolling back through a chat on a phone no longer closes the keyboard. Nudging the transcript to reread the line you were writing dismissed it every time, because any touch at all counted as a dismissal. It now goes only on a deliberate downward flick, so reading keeps the keyboard however far you scroll, and a drag back toward the newest message never dismisses.
- The chat send button works on the first tap with the keyboard up. The first tap used to only close the keyboard, and the layout shifting as it left meant the tap landed where the button no longer was. Return also goes back to inserting a line break rather than sending. Sending now takes the transcript to the newest message, so what you wrote and the reply to it are on screen instead of left behind wherever you had scrolled to.
- Attaching a file in chat no longer drops the keyboard, and each row goes where it says it will. The attachment button opened the browser's own picker, which closes the keyboard and cannot be aimed at a source — all three choices led to the same file browser. In the Istota iOS app the rows now open the photo library, the camera and the document picker directly; elsewhere they behave as they did.
- Empty date fields showed as blank slivers on iPhone. iOS draws nothing at all in a date input with no value, so the health history range filter rendered as two thin boxes with nothing in them.
- GPS location tracking works under the combined local launcher. The webhook sat one path segment deeper than the documented address and its token list was never loaded, so a correctly-configured phone was refused every time. Only the single-machine install was affected; a server deployment runs the receiver as its own service.
- The estimated-tax page read back a larger withholding figure than the one entered. Year-to-date withholding is projected to a full year for the calculation and has to be unprojected to be shown again, and that was done against calendar quarters rather than the periods the tax method actually uses — so outside the first quarter the field disagreed with what had been typed into it. A missing tax configuration also shows its own empty state now, instead of a bare "API error: 404".
- The Reports tab in Money lands on the report it marks as current. It opened the income statement while the sub-navigation showed cash flow as selected, so the page you got and the page shown as chosen were never the same one.
- The Reports and Business sub-navigation in Money collapses into a dropdown on a phone, as the main navigation already did, instead of running off the side of the screen. Toolbars that hold a search box also sit level now, rather than the box standing a head taller than the dropdown beside it.
- A video inside a feed post fits the card and can be played. Clips were laid out at their own full resolution, so one ran off the side of the card and of the reader; a clip authored as a silent looping GIF-style video was also stored with no way to start it, and arrived as a black rectangle. Both are fixed, and the Images toggle hides body videos along with the pictures. Posts already on file pick this up the next time their feed refreshes.
